\spacedallcaps

Lattice Theory in Multi-Agent Systems

\spacedlowsmallcaps

Hans Riess

Doctor of Philosophy

Electrical and Systems Engineering

University of Pennsylvania

2022

Hans Riess:
Lattice Theory in Multi-Agent Systems,
The Tarski Laplacian & Applications, © 2022

for my dad who would

on napkins and on airplanes

teach me algebra

Abstract

SIn this thesis, we argue that (order-) lattice-based multi-agent information systems constitute a broad class of networked multi-agent systems in which relational data is passed between nodes. Mathematically modeled as lattice-valued sheaves, we initiate a discrete Hodge theory with a Laplace operator, analogous to the graph Laplacian and the graph connection Laplacian, acting on assignments of data to the nodes of a Tarski sheaf. The Hodge-Tarski theorem (the main theorem) relates the fixed point theory of this operator, called the Tarski Laplacian in deference to the Tarski Fixed Point Theorem, to the global sections (consistent global states) of the sheaf. We present novel applications to signal processing and multi-agent semantics and supply a plethora of examples throughout.

Part I   Mathematical Background

†
†
margin:

1

Introduction

In this thesis we offer a novel approach to understanding multi-agent systems from a lattice-theoretic point of view, a stark contrast to modern approaches relying on spectral graph theory
[
chung1997spectral
]
, dynamical systems
[
ghrist2022applied
]
, and control theory
[
chen1984linear
]
.

1
Multi-Agent Systems

A system is something so fundamental it is difficult to define. While we will even not attempt to define systems here, we will define multi-agent systems as a special class of systems consisting of many interacting components called
agents
. Agents are differentiated from ordinary components of systems such as the environment because agents are typically assumed to possess some degree of autonomy, intelligence, or communication capabilities, likely all three. Consider just a few examples of multi-agent systems.

1.

Wireless Communication
. Communication systems are naturally multi-agent systems. Agents send/receive
messages
. More elaborate multi-agent systems usually rely on a communication subsystem in order to gain information from other agents in the system.

2.

Swarm robotics
. Multiple (usually simple) robots (e.g. quadrotor UAVs) collaborate to perform tasks such as surveillance, payload transportation, and more
[
abdelkader2021aerial
]
. Swarms, it is widely held, outperforms smaller groups of more sophisticated robots due to advantages of scalability and robustness.

3.

Sensor networks
. Multiple agents, equipped with one or more sensors, gather information about the environment or other agents inside or outside of the system. Applications include wearable devices, threat detection, industrial monitoring, and environmental conditions.

4.

Firms
. Firms compete with directly with other firms, exchanging information via prices as well as through other methods such as collusion, acquisition, or mergers.

In multi-agent systems, heterogeneous streams of information, possibly encoded in various types of data structures, are collected, processed, and exchanged among the various agents in the system. Consider the following examples of information processing tasks performed by multi-agent systems.

1.

Consensus.
Consensus is a broad category of information processing that aims for every agent to eventually come to an agreement on a particular state or quantity of interest that depends on the state of all the agents. Some examples include flocking
[
tanner2007flocking
]
and distributed formation control
[
fax2004information
]
in swarm robotic systems, synchronization, and rendezvous in space
[
cortes2006robust
]
and time
[
nejad2009max
]
. A consensus algorithm, also called a consensus protocol, is a rule of interaction between agents specifying the exchange of information between a given agent and all of its neighbors in the network
[
olfati2007consensus
]
.

2.

Collaborative Filtering
. Collaborative filtering is a general technique of filtering information from multiple sources facilitated by “interactions” between agents. The most popular use-case of collaborative filtering is recommendation systems which take into account both the preferences of an individual agent as well as similar agents, neighbors, in a graph. To this end, graph signal processing
[
ortega2018graph
]
has shown to be an effective tool for rating prediction
[
huang2018rating
]
.

3.

Information Fusion
. Information fusion, also known as
data fusion
, is the task of integrating data from multiple sources in heterogeneous formats. In one view
[
khaleghi2013multisensor
]
, data fusion is the study of the transformation of various sources of information into a representation that can be interpreted by a machine or human.

In each of the above information processing tasks, local information is aggregated in order to obtain global information. Sheaf theory
[
bredon2012sheaf
,
curry2014sheaves
]
, it is our view, is an appropriate context to study such data relationships. Sheaves are data structures supplying mathematical rigor to the soft questions such as
is local data consistent?
,
do globally consistent assignments of data even exist?
In the agriculturally inspired nomenclature, a sheaf is a fixation of stalks (e.g. vector spaces, sets, lattices, etc.) to a base (e.g. graph, hypergraph, simplicial complex, manifold). A network sheaf (Definition
5.1
) is a sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
based over an undirected graph
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
specified by the data of objects (e.g. vectors paces) over nodes and edges called
stalks
, and maps preserving the structure of objects (e.g. linear transformations) called
restriction maps
. If
i
and
j
are nodes and
ij
is an edge, stalks are denoted
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)
,
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j)
, and
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)
, and restriction maps denoted
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij):\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)\to\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)
.

Sheaves facilitate the identification of consistent assignments of data called
global sections
(Definition
5.3
). We argue that global sections

\displaystyle\Gamma(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{\underset{\textit{assignments of data}}{\mathbf{x}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \underset{\textit{agreement along restriction maps}}{\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})=\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})}\quad\forall ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\Bigl\}

are a natural extension or generalization of consensus.
1
1

1

If every restriction map is the identity, then we recover consensus exactly.
In Chapter
, we discover a notion of consensus in multi-agent epistemic logic which we could call
semantic consensus
.

2
Lattice Thoery

Lattices are a class of ordered sets with two “merging” operations called
meet
and
join
. As ordered sets, finite lattices can be visualized with
Hasse diagrams
, directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). The nodes of a Hasse diagram are the elements of the ordered set. If
x\preccurlyeq y
, and there is no other element between
x
and
y
, we draw a directed edge from
x
to
y
. Orders are elementary, but lattices are less known. There may be a historical explanation.

Lattice theory
[
birkhoff1940lattice
,
davey2002introduction
,
gratzer2002general
,
roman2008lattices
]
is a multi-faceted field of mathematics, with a rich history
[
bilova2001lattice
]
. However, as a field, lattice theory never fully matured, perhaps unjustly so
[
rota1997many
]
:

Never in the history of mathematics has a mathematical theory been the object of such vociferous vituperations as lattice theory. Dedekind, Jonsson, Kurosh, Malcev, Ore, von Neumann, Tarski, and most prominently Garrett Birkhoff have contributed a new vision of mathematics, a vision that has
been cursed by a conjunction of misunderstanding, resentment, and raw prejudice.

Lattices, as a mathematical concept, is a relaxation of a boolean algebra
[
boole1847mathematical
]
. To describe these structures, it was Klein who originally coined a term,
verband
, which loosely translates from German as “association.” However, Garret Birkhoff, popularized the English nomenclature
[
bilova2001lattice
]
, “lattice,” due to the fact (with some speculation) that the Hasse diagrams of lattices often resemble lattices of the garden variety (pardon the pun!).

We recall this obscure history to highlight the fact that “lattice” has an unfortunate ambiguous meaning. To a physicist, a lattice is (likely) a discreet subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(Figure
1
). To a network-scientist
[
watts2004six
]
, a lattice is (likely) an undirected graph whose nodes uniformly follow a grid pattern and whose edges are drawn according to (unique!) nearest neighbors. We call these objects
integer lattice
or
lattice graphs
to differentiate them from an
order lattices
.

Figure 1:
(Left) integer lattice; (right) order lattice.

Thus, lattice theory, as a subject, is certainly conflicted in its identity. Brought about as a weakening of the axioms boolean logic (e.g. no longer requiring negation and distributivity), on one hand, lattice theory belongs to logic. An argument could also be made that lattice theory belongs to combinatorics
[
rota1964foundations
]
. In our view, lattice theory, while establishing connections between the areas mentioned and more
[
rota1997many
]
, belongs to algebra, the general study of symbol manipulation. For one, a lattice is a universal algebra
[
sankappanavar1981course
]
, roughly, a set equipped with a collection of
n
-ary operations satisfying equational identities. Another compelling reason to highlight the algebraic side of lattice theory is that lattices of subgroups of a group, a collection of symmetries, are perhaps the first example studied in lattice theory. Permutations on
n
elements form a group. You can reverse permutations by sending
j
to
i
, as opposed to sending
i
to
j
. Furthermore, you can apply permutations repeatedly and it doesn’t matter where you place parentheses. The group of permutations on
4
elements, a seemingly benign structure, has a rather intricate lattice of subgroups (Figure
2
). Substructures share non-trivial relationships, even in such a simple example.

Figure 2:
Hasse diagram of the lattice of subgroups of
S_{4}
.

x

Lattices function both as ordered sets and algebraic structures. Suppose
x,y\in\mathbf{L}
, an ordered set with
\preccurlyeq
, then
x\vee y
(join) is the least upper bound (if it exists) and
x\wedge y
(meet) is the greatest lower bound (again, it exists). On one hand, a lattice is an ordered set such that the least upper bounds and greatest lower bounds are guaranteed to exist (Definition
2.6
). On the other hand, a lattice is, a set with binary operations
\vee
and
\wedge
satisfying axioms (Definition
2.5
). Elementary examples of lattices include the following.

Examples 1.1

(Lattices)
.

1.

Subsets of an arbitrary set with the operations union and intersection. Alternatively, subsets with an inclusion relation.

2.

Subgroups (subspaces) of a group (vector space) with product (sum) and intersection (Figure
2
). Alternatively, subgroups (subspaces) with an inclusion relation.

3.

Partitions of an arbitrary set with coarsest common refinement under refinement. Alternatively, partitions under the refinement relation.

4.

Truth values
\mathbf{B}=\{0,1\}
with
\mathtt{OR}
/
\mathtt{AND}
. Alternatively,
\mathbf{B}
with the order
0<1
.

Other quite general examples are embedded in other lattices such as powersets and partitions, including subpartitions (Example
), and lattices characterizing formal concepts
[
wille1982restructuring
]
and fuzzy logic
[
belohlavek1999fuzzy
]
. Less known is a “lattice theory of information” in which the information content (relative entropy) of a random variable establishes a lattice order. Joins and meets have interesting interpretations (Example
).

This reoccurring duality between order and algebraicity inspires a new outlook on multi-agent systems. Suppose a system consists of
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
agents whose communication patterns are modeled by a graph
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
with
{\mathcal{V}_{G}}=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. Data collected in a lattice by individual agents models relational information about the system. On the other hand, because of the duality, this data is amalgamated with data stored by neighboring agents via the binary operations, meet and join.

As a point of clarification, it is not our goal to develop a general theory of multi-agent systems, but to make a case that certain mathematical tools are fundamental in modeling multi-agent information systems. To this end, it is our hope that the theory of lattice-valued sheaves (Chapter
6
) and sheaf Laplacians (Chapter
7
) posited in this manuscript inspire novel information and control systems.

3
Survey of Contributions

The
Tarski Laplacian
is the key construction and contribution. The Tarski Laplacian was first introduced
[
ghrist2022cellular
]
in an attempt to establish a Hodge theory and thus define cohomology of cellular sheaves valued in lattices. The difficulty in defining cohomolgoy is that the construction of the cellular sheaf cochain complex initiated by Shepard
[
shepard1985cellular
]
breaks apart. To make a long story short, meets and joins lack inverses. Nonetheless, some key computational and theoretical construction are still possible in
homological algebra

[
grandis2013homological
]
. Cohomology of mathematical objects such as simplicial complexes, topological spaces, or groups is often helpful in classifying properties of a structure. We would expect cohomology of lattice-valued sheaves would have ties to the behavior of the information systems they model. In the theory of cellular sheaves
[
curry2014sheaves
]
, sheaf cohomology
H^{0}
classifies the global sections. Global sections are identified as a lattice of fixed points via the Tarski Laplacian (Hodge-Tarski Theorem, Theorem
7.1
).

Homology of chain complexes in semiexact categories (the category of lattices and Galois connections is semiexact) as well as exact sequences and the snake lemma were established Marco Grandis
[
grandis2013homological
]
. We suspect a proper theory of sheaf cohomology of
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
-valued sheaves would require either a modified notion of projective or injective resolutions of sheaves
[
bredon2012sheaf
]
, and, thus, a notion of projective or injective (complete) lattices. With the program to develop a cohomology theory for lattice-valued sheaves supplanted by novel applications of the Tarski Laplacian, our efforts are contained in the following manuscript
[
ghrist2022cellular
]
.

It has been suggested that sheaf theory simultaneously straddles both the axes of algebra/geometry and structure/obstruction
[
ghrist2021laplacians
]
. Lattice-valued sheaf theory, we place in the second quadrant of this helpful philosophical model (Figure
3
). While lattices are pure algebraic structures, in Chapter
7
, we introduce geometric notations such as Laplace operators and even parallel transport.

Figure 3:
A philosophical model of applied sheaf theory: (from left to right) Leray sheaves
[
curry2018dualities
]
, lattice-valued sheaves
[
ghrist2022cellular
]
, evasion path sheaves
[
ghrist2017positive
]
, path optimization sheaves
[
moy2020path
]
, Reeb cosheaves
[
de2016categorified
]
, spectral sheaves
[
hansen2019toward
]
, and (pseudo)-metric space sheaves
[
robinson2020assignments
]
.

From the standpoint of category theory, lattice-valued sheaves over (incidence) posets
are a categorification
[
baez1998categorification
]
of sheaves valued in an abelian category. Abelian groups, for instance, are replaced with lattices of subgroups (categories) and homomorphisms are replaced with Galois connections (adjunctions). This fortuitous lifting of classical sheaf theory to lattice theory is not without a price. When a functor from a poset into the category of complete lattices and Galois connections factors through the functor sending an object to its lattice of (normal) subobjects, we cannot, in general, recover the homology of the abelian sheaf, from the lattice-valued sheaf
[
ghrist2022cellular
, Section 4.3]
.

The Tarski Laplacian is a local operator that acts on assignments of data to a sheaf of lattices. The Tarski Laplacian is called a “Laplacian” for a number of reasons. Primarily, the Hodge-Tarski Theorem identifies the global sections of a lattice-valued sheaf with fixed points. We offer a deep connection to the graph connection Laplacian
[
singer2012vector
]
based on a notion of parallel transport between local sections of a network sheaf recently introduced
[
bodnar2022neural
]
. We hope the Tarski Laplacian can establish connections to information geometry
[
li2021transport
]
and representation theory
[
krishnan2020invertibility
]
.

Dynamical systems on network sheaves with coefficients in Hilbert spaces (e.g.
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) were initiated by Hansen
[
hansen2019distributed
,
hansen2021opinion
,
hansenconnections
]
. We introduce a dynamical system

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L\mathbf{x}[t]\wedge\mathbf{x}[t]

called the
heat equation
with a time-varying version called the
gossip equation
(
3
). Beyond diffusion dynamics, saddle-point dynamics, as well as other local dynamics based on simple aggregation rules are of interest. In the narrow case of approximation of the constant sheaf (Definition
7.2
), consensus algorithms are a byproduct of diffusion dynamics driven by the Tarski Laplacian.

We spend the better part of Chapter
8
comparing approaches to lattice signal processing, as well as providing our own. We identify the convolution defined by Püschel
[
puschel2021discrete
]
as an algebraic signal model and describe the relationship between two algebraic signal models corresponding to signals on lattices. In the final chapter, after discussing a class of networked model checking problems, we present applications of the Tarski Laplacian to semantics, offering novel models of knowledge diffusion/consensus motivated by epistemic logic
[
fagin2004reasoning
]
.

4
Related Work

We make no attempt at a comprehensive literature review, focusing on a few relevant topics, instead.

4.1
Quantum logic

In physics, Birkhoff and von Neumann proposed lattice theory as a logical model for quantum mechanics
[
birkhoff1936logic
]
. Their work is based on the following observation. Suppose
H
is a Hilbert space, then the set of closed subspaces of
H
forms an (orthocomplemented) lattice under the closure of subspace sums (join) and intersection (meet). These subspaces are in one-to-one correspondence with projections which von Neumann reasoned could be viewed as quantum observables
[
von2018mathematical
]
. However, their approach did not stand the test of time, due to Bell’s Theorem being experimentally disproved
[
aspect1981experimental
]
. Consequently, monoidal categories/string diagrams
[
coecke2018picturing
]
and linear logic
[
girard1987linear
]
, the logic of quantum information theory, are more fashionable these days.

4.2
Supermodular games

Economists have applied lattice theory to a particular class of
n
-person games (multi-agent system). A real-valued function on a lattice is
supermodular
if

\displaystyle f(x\vee y)+f(x\wedge y)\geqslant f(x)+f(y)\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbf{L}.

Then, a
supermodular game
consists of players
i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
each with a lattice of possible strategies
\mathbf{L}_{i}
and a utility function

\displaystyle u_{i}(x_{i},\mathbf{x}_{-i}):\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{L}_{i}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle x_{i}\in\mathbf{L}_{i},\quad\mathbf{x}_{-i}\in\prod_{j\neq i}\mathbf{L}_{i}\quad i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}

such that
u_{i}(-,\mathbf{x}_{-i})
is supermodular and
u_{i}
satisfies an additional property of
increasing differences
. Then, every
n
-person supermodular game has a Nash equilibrium
[
topkis1978minimizing
]
(see also
[
vohra2004advanced
, Chapter 7]
). It was later shown by Zhou that if each strategy lattice is complete, the set of Nash equilibrium forms a complete lattice
[
zhou1994set
]
. While pertinent to problems in economics such as Bertrand competition
[
edgeworth1925pure
]
, supermodular games have found practical use in wireless communication networks, with applications to energy-efficient power allocation
[
liu2018supermodular
]
, power control
[
altman2003supermodular
]
, and interference compensation
[
huang2006distributed
]
. Submodular functions on a lattice, real-valued functions satisfying

\displaystyle f(x\vee y)+f(x\wedge y)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle f(x)+f(y)\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbf{L},

and algorithms to minimize them were introduced by Topkis
[
topkis1978minimizing
]
. Unsupervised learning problems such as principal component analysis (PCA) and generalized PCA, have been reformulated as constrained submodular maximization problems on the lattice of spaces of
\mathbb{R}^{d}

[
nakashima2019subspace
]
, as well as meta-learning
[
adibi2020submodular
]
.

4.3
Discrete event systems

In discrete event systems
[
cassandras2008introduction
]
, an area of control theory, Galois connections model the triggering of events. A discrete event system is a transition system (Chapter
) with discrete states and transitions triggered by events. Linear systems
[
chen1984linear
]
and their properties (e.g. controlability, observability), are well-understood. In general, discrete event systems are less tractable, however, in the past two decades, much headway has been made by representing discrete-event systems with square matrices with elements in the following lattice.

Let
\mathbb{R}_{\max}
denote the extended reals
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
.
\mathbb{R}_{\max}
is a (complete) lattice under the operations
\max
and
\min
which we denote with
\vee
and
\wedge
.
\mathbb{R}_{\max}\setminus\{-\infty,\infty\}
has the structure of a group under addition (Definition
2.8
). Replacing the “times” with “plus” and “times” in an ordinary euclidean space with “max” or “min” one obtains a
max-plus vector space

\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}
, an example of a
weighted lattice

[
maragos2017dynamical
]
. As in ordinary linear algebra, matrix multiplication defines a transformation. If
A\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m\times m}
is a matrix and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}
is a vector, then matrix multiplication is defined in two dual ways

\displaystyle(A\ovee\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{m}a_{ij}+x_{j},

\displaystyle(A\owedge\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}a_{ij}+x_{j}.

For illustration, consider the following discrete event system. Suppose a set of
\{1,2,\dots,m\}
events begin at time
x_{i}[t],i\in\{1,2,\dots,m\}
after
t\in\mathbb{N}
rounds. Suppose, an event
j
can only begin after another even
j
has terminated (e.g. ironing a shirt after drying). Then, we say
i
transitions from
j
. With considerations of system design, waiting times depend not only on the ongoing event
i
but also on the target event
j
. Let
a_{ij}
denote the duration of time between the start of
i
and the (subsequent) start of
j
. If
j
does not depend on process
i
, then set
a_{ij}=-\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle x_{i}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{j=1}^{m}x_{i}[t]+a_{ij}\quad i=1,2,\dots,m

is the new start time of event
i
.
Let
A\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}
be the matrix with entries
a_{ij}
, then the global dynamics are written in the following form

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A\ovee\mathbf{x}[t].

Suppose in two interacting discrete event systems, events are collected from the same set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. If
\mathbf{x}[t],\mathbf{y}[t]\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}
are vectors of event times after
t
rounds of System 1 and System 2, respectively, and
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
are matrices of transition times, then the
synchronization problem
asks whether
(\mathbf{x}[0],\mathbf{y}[0])
can be chosen so that there exists a
t_{0}
such that
\mathbf{x}[t]=\mathbf{y}[t]
for all
t\geqslant t_{0}
. Clearly, this is equivalent to solving the system

\displaystyle A\ovee\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B\ovee\mathbf{y}

for
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}
.

Cunninghame-Green & Butkovic proposed a solution to the synchronization problem called the
alternating method

[
cuninghame2003equation
]
. Suppose
A^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m\times m}
with entries
[a^{\dagger}_{ij}]=-a_{ji}
. Then, the maps

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle A\ovee\mathbf{x},

\displaystyle A^{\ast}\owedge\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\mapsfrom

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}

form a Galois connection (Example
3.1
). Each iteration of the alternating method is equivalent to applying the Tarski Laplacian (Chapter
7
)

\displaystyle L(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(A^{\dagger}\owedge\left(B\ovee\mathbf{y}\right),B^{\dagger}\owedge\left(A\ovee\mathbf{x}\right)\right)

(1)

on a particular “sheaf” over the graph
\bullet-\bullet
(Figure

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\dagger}\owedge\mathbf{z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\dagger}\owedge\mathbf{z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\ovee\mathbf{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\ovee\mathbf{y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\dagger}}

Figure 4:
A max-plus sheaf modeling synchronization of discrete-event systems
[
cuninghame2003equation
]
.

Synchronization is equivalent to being a section of the sheaf. Therefore, by the Hodge-Tarski Theorem (Theorem
7.1
), synchronization is also
equivalent to the condition

\displaystyle A^{\dagger}\owedge\left(B\ovee\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle B^{\dagger}\owedge\left(A\ovee\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq\mathbf{y}

for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{m}
.

4.4
Consensus

Lattice consensus algorithms in the networked multi-agent setting (Algorithm
1
, Algorithm
2
, Algorithm
3
) are novel, but consensus on lattices, while obscure, broadly construed, is not. While we have discussed several notions of consensus in multi-agent systems, consensus has other connotations in both computer science as well as economics.

Reaching agreement among remote processes (agents) is a fundamental problem in distributed computing. In a consensus protocol, each process
i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
receives or seeds an input register
x_{i}
, sends and receives messages to other processes, and, eventually, must determine an output register
y_{i}
coinciding with the output registers of all other processes. Unfortunately, it has been shown that, roughly, no asynchronous consensus protocol can tolerate (i.e. not affect the output) a single fault. For instance, if a process is unexpectedly removed from the system
[
fischer1985impossibility
]
, this would be considered a
fault
.

Now, suppose input and output registers are collected in a lattice (Definition
2.5
). Then, lattice agreement is defined to be a decision
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
such that
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a chain and the following inequality holds for all
i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}.

Lattice agreement, centralized branch-and-bound algorithms, have been shown to satisfy fault-tolerance in a variety of settings
[
zheng2018lattice
,
zheng2021byzantine
,
zheng2020byzantine
]
, unlike lattice consensus.

In the theory of social choice
[
arrow2012social
]
, consensus is roughly equivalent to “choosing in groups”
[
munger2015choosing
]
. At the highest level of generality, if
X
is a finite set of alternatives (choices), a consensus rule is a map

\chi:\bigcup_{k>1}X^{k}\to\wp(X)\setminus\emptyset

sending a tuple
\boldsymbol{\pi}
of arbitrary individual choices called a profile to a nonempty set of group choices
chi(\boldsymbol{\pi})

[
barthelemy1991formal
]
. Suppose we fix the number of voters (agents) to be
n
and replace
X
with a lattice
\mathbf{L}
, then a
(latticial) consensus function
2
2

2

If such a function is monotone and sends the top (bottom) profile to the top (bottom) element of
\mathbf{L}
, then it has been called a
aggregation function

[
botur2018generating
]
, although “aggregation function” is sometimes synonymous with a general consensus function.

[
barnett1995social
]
or
aggregation function

[
janowitz2016aggregation
,
leclerc2013aggregation
]
is a map

\chi:\mathbf{L}^{n}\to\mathbf{L}.

Lattices frequently arise in consensus settings in social choice. For instance,
\mathbf{L}
could be the lattice of (transitive, reflexive) preference relations on
X
or a lattice of choice functions
[
monjardet2004lattices
]
, maps
c:\wp(X)\to\wp(X)
such that
c(A)\subseteq A
for all
A\subseteq X
. In the nomenclature, a consensus function
\chi
is a
meet-projection
if there is a subset
N\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
such that
\chi(\boldsymbol{\pi})=\bigwedge_{i\in N}\pi_{i}
and, in particular, a
Pareto consensus function
if
N=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

A third interpretation of lattice consensus comes about in the general science of classification/taxonomy. In evolutionary biology, phylogenetic trees can be inferred from DNA sequences
[
nascimento2017biologist
]
. Having several candidates for the evolutionary tree of a particular organism, consensus is one approach to determining an aggregate phylogenetic tree
[
bryant2003classification
]
. In the same spirit, when an ensemble of hierarchical clustering algorithms produces various candidate dendrograms, consensus on the outputs selects a single representative tree
[
neumann1986lattice
]
.

Last, but not least, max/min consensus aims to compute the aggregate max or min of a signal on the nodes of a network. In some settings, maximum and minimum are approximated
[
tahbaz2006one
]
, or algorithms for reaching consensus on general functions on the inputs were proposed
[
cortes2008distributed
]
. Max consensus was also framed as a max-plus-linear system
[
nejad2009max
]
. Useful applications of min/max consensus and, arguably, meet/join consensus, include decentralized leader election
[
borsche2010leader
]
and minimum-time rendezvous
[
nejad2009max
]
.

4.5
Applied sheaf theory

Applied sheaf theory likely originated in the 1970s. Sheaves were argued to be an intuitive way of thinking about certain image segmentation problems
[
bajcsy1973computer
]
. In the 1990s, the following audacious claim was made by Goguen, who applied sheaf theory to electrical circuitry
[
goguen1992sheaf
]
:

The sheaf condition appears to be satisfied by the behaviours of all naturally arising systems from computing science. This “Sheaf Hypothesis” is similar to the Church-Turing thesis, that all intuitively computable functions are computable in the precise sense of Turning machines.

Sheaf theory, as a field of applied mathematics, is not yet mature. Some successes include sheaves as a tool for integrating sensors
[
robinson2017sheaves
]
, filter design
[
robinson2014topological
]
, path optimzation and routing
[
cormen2022introduction
,
moy2020path
]
. The Greatest triumph of applied sheaf may be an ongoing program in the foundations of topological data analysis
[
ghrist2008barcodes
]
. The theory of both persistent homology
[
kashiwara2018persistent
,
curry2014sheaves
,
macpherson2021persistent
]
and Reeb graphs
[
de2016categorified
]
has much benefited from a sheaf-theoretic viewpoint. In most settings, the data category of the sheaf (i.e. what lives “upstairs”) is the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces and the space is a graph. In this manuscript, we study sheaves over graphs valued in
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
, the data category of complete lattices (Chapter
6
). We briefly review adjacent work on sheaves valued in nonabelian categories.

In quantum mechanics, sheaves have been used to model non-locality: mysterious interactions between fundamental particles, presumably occurring faster than the speed of light
[
abramsky2011sheaf
]
. Contextuality, a version of non-locality, is formulated as a sheaf of events, a sheaf in the data category of sets. Cohomology is undefinable here, hence, obstructions to locality, computed as cohomology in degree one, cannot be explicitly calculated from the sheaf of events. One approach is to extend the sheaf of events to a sheaf of abelian groups factoring through the free functor sending a set to its free abelian group. one disadvantage of this approach is that sections may appear that weren’t there before. Alternatively, Čech cohomology was recently defined for sheaves of semimodules
[
montanhano2021characterization
]
.

In our opinion, the most significant development in applied sheaf theory is the introduction of sheaf Laplacians
[
hansen2019toward
]
. Thus far, sheaf Laplacians have inspired applications in opinion dynamics
[
hansen2021opinion
]
, distributed optimization
[
hansen2019distributed
]
, sheaf learning
[
hansen2019learning
]
, and graph neural networks
[
bodnar2022neural
,
barbero2022sheaf
]
.

†
†
margin:

2

Lattices

5
From Relations to Posets

Given sets
X
and
Y
, a binary relation is simply a subset
\mathcal{R}\subseteq X\times Y
. Sometimes we write
x\mathcal{R}y
whenever
(x,y)\in\mathcal{R}
. Suppose
\mathcal{R}\subseteq X\times Y
and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq Y\times Z
. We can compose relations as follows:

\mathcal{S}\circ\mathcal{R}=\{(x,z)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists\penalty\ y\in Y\penalty\ \text{such that}\penalty\ x\mathcal{R}y,\penalty\ y\mathcal{S}z\}.

We may also take unions
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
and intersections
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of relations. Relations have duals. Suppose
\mathcal{R}\in X\times Y
is a relation. Then
\mathcal{R}^{\dagger}
is the relation
\{(y,x)\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\mathcal{R}y\}
.

Example 2.1

(Databases)
.

Suppose
X
is a set of instances and
Y
is a set of attributes. We set
x\mathcal{R}y
if object
x
has attribute
y
. Most of the time, attributes are not binary labels. One way to accommodate this situation to convert these labels to integers. Then, a multi-valued relation is a map
\mathcal{R}:X\times Y\to\mathbb{N}
. Then, we define a filtration of relations

\mathcal{R}_{\leqslant m}=\{(x,y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{R}(x,y)\leqslant m\}.

(2)

This sequence of relations is a filtration in the sense that
\mathcal{R}_{\leqslant m}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\leqslant m^{\prime}}
whenever
m\leqslant m^{\prime}
.

Motivated by the preceding example, it is convenient to have notation for the set of attributes that is related to a given object. Let
\mathcal{R}\subseteq X\times Y
be a relation and
\sigma\subseteq X
. Then, the
intent
of
\sigma
, denoted
{\sigma}^{\uparrow}
, is the set
\{y\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\mathcal{R}y\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ x\in\sigma\}
. Conversely, the
extent
of a
\tau\subseteq Y
, denoted
{\tau}^{\downarrow}
, is the set
\{x\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\mathcal{R}y\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ y\in\tau\}
.

Whenever
X=Y
, we say
\mathcal{R}
is an
endorelation
(or simply a relation from context). We denote the set of endorelations on
X
with
\Rel{X}
. Endorelations are the same as directed graphs. For an
x\in X
, the intent
{x}^{\uparrow}
is sometimes called the
reachability set
or
children
of
x
. The extent
{x}^{\downarrow}
is called the
predecessor set
or
parents
of
x
. Several properties of endorelations are of note.

Definition 2.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}\in\Rel{X}
. Then,
\mathcal{R}
is

1.

Transitive
if
x\mathcal{R}y
,
y\mathcal{R}z
implies
x\mathcal{R}z
,

2.

Reflexive
if
x\mathcal{R}x
,

3.

Anti-symmetric
if
x\mathcal{R}y
and
y\mathcal{R}x
implies
x=y
,

4.

Symmetric
if
x\mathcal{R}y
and
y\mathcal{R}x
,

5.

Serial
if for every
x\in X
, there exist
y\in X
such that
x\mathcal{R}y
,

6.

Euclidean
if for every
x,y,z\in X
,
x\mathcal{R}y
and
x\mathcal{R}z
implies
y\mathcal{R}z
,

7.

Connex
if for every
x,y\in X
,
x\mathcal{R}y
or
y\mathcal{R}x
.

A
equivalence relation
is a relation
\sim\penalty\ \in\penalty\ \Rel{X}
satisfying transitivity, reflexivity and symmetry. It is well known that equivalence relations are in one-to-one correspondence between equivalence relations and partitions. A partition of
X
is a collection of subsets
\pi=\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that
\bigcup\pi=X
and
X_{i}\cap X_{j}=\emptyset
for all
i\neq j
. Let
\Pi\left(X\right)
denote the set of partitions of
X
. It is often convenient to represent a partition e.g.
\pi\in\Pi\left(\{1,2,3,4\}\right)
as
\pi=\{12|3|4\}
.

Example 2.2

(Semantics)
.

This example serves as a prelude to Chapter
. Suppose
S
is set of states of a system. For instance,
S
could be units of time, or, in a multi-agent system, each agent could have local states
S_{i}
, for instance, detection states
\{pos,neg\}
in a sensor network, or message statuses
\{sent,received\}
. In the local paradigm, global states are a prodcut of local states
S=\prod_{i}S_{i}
. A
Kripke relation
is an endorelation
\mathcal{K}\in\Rel(S)
. A Kripke relation defines a “possible worlds model” upon which various multi-agent logics
[
fagin2004reasoning
]
are built. For instance, given a state
s
, the reachability set
s^{\uparrow}=\{t\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\mathcal{K}t\}
constitutes set of states for which a given proposition must be true in order that the proposition be “especially” true at state
s
.

Definition 2.2
.

A
poset
(
p
artially
o
rdered
set
)
(\mathrm{P},\preccurlyeq)
is a set
X
with a transitive, reflexive and anti-symmetric binary relation
\preccurlyeq
.

We often omit the order
\preccurlyeq
in
(\mathrm{P},\preccurlyeq)
when it is clear from context. If we remove the anti-symmetry requirement, we say
\mathrm{P}
is a preordered set or
proset
. We write
x\prec y
if
x\preccurlyeq y
and
x\neq y
. The ordering
\prec
is called a
strict order
.

Before getting to far along, we describe a standard way to visualize posets which will require a definition. We say that an element
y
of
\mathrm{P}

covers

x
written
x\lessdot y
if there does not exist
z\in\mathrm{P}
with
x\prec z\prec y
. We form a covering (endo)relation
\lessdot
depicting the elements that cover each other. We can draw the covering relation as a directed graph called a
Hasse diagram
. For historical reasons, we do not draw arrows because arrows always point from the bottom to the top of the page. Paths in the hasse diagram correspond to chains. Transitivity and reflexivity is implicit. We do not draw self-loops or arrows that realize transitivity, but they are there (Figure
5
.)

Figure 5:
Covering relation (Hasse diagram) v.s. entire partial order.

5.1
Subsets of posets

Several types of subsets of
\mathrm{P}
are of interest. An
upset
is a subset
U\subseteq\mathrm{P}
with the property

x\in U,\penalty\ y\succcurlyeq x\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ y\in U.

Similarly, a
downset

D
is a subset
D\subseteq\mathrm{P}
with the property

x\in D,\penalty\ y\preccurlyeq x,\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ y\in D.

A
principal upset
is generated by a single
x\in\mathrm{P}
:
\uparrow\!{x}=\{y\in\mathrm{P}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\succcurlyeq x\}
. A
principal downset
is generated by a single
x\in\mathrm{P}
:
\downarrow x=\{y\in\mathrm{P}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\preccurlyeq x\}
.

An
interval
in a poset
\mathrm{P}
is a subset
I\subseteq\mathrm{P}
with the convexity property that for every
x,y\in I
, if
x\preccurlyeq z\preccurlyeq y
in
\mathrm{P}
, then
z\in\mathrm{P}
also. An interval is
closed
if it is the form
\left[x,y\right]=\{z\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\preccurlyeq z\preccurlyeq y\}
. We denote the set of closed intervals in a poset
\mathbb{I}{\mathrm{P}}
. A
chain
is a subset
C\subseteq\mathrm{P}
such that
C
is totally ordered: for every
x,y\in C
, either
x\preccurlyeq y
, or
y\preccurlyeq x
(or both). Similarly, an
antichain
is a subset
A\subseteq\mathrm{P}
such that for every
x,y\in A
, neither
x\preccurlyeq y
nor
y\preccurlyeq x
(written
x\parallel y
). The
height
of
\mathrm{P}
is the (path) length of the maximal chain. The
width
of
\mathrm{P}
is the cardinality of the maximal antichain.

A subset
V\subseteq\mathrm{P}
is
directed
if for every
x,y\in\mathrm{P}
, there is a
z
such that
z\succcurlyeq x,z\succcurlyeq y
. A subset
F\subseteq\mathrm{P}
is
filtered
if for every
x,y\in\mathrm{P}
, there is a
z
such that
z\preccurlyeq x,y
.

5.2
Convergence

When computing with lattices, it is useful to have a notion when chains “converge” in both a finite and trans-finite sense.

Definition 2.3
.

(\mathrm{P},\preccurlyeq)
satisfies the descending chain condition (DCC) if every every chain
C\subseteq P
has a minimum element. Similarly,
\mathrm{P}
satisfies the ascending chain condition (ACC) if every chain
C
has a maximum element.

A fortuitous sufficient condition for satisfying DCC (dualy, ACC) is a grading. A poset
\mathrm{P}
is
graded
if there is a map

r:\mathrm{P}\to\mathbb{N}

(3)

such that
r(x)<r(y)
whenever
x\preccurlyeq y
(strictly monotone) and
r(y)=r(x)+1
whenever
x\lessdot y
. A grading on a poset is a particularly restrictive topological sorting of the Hasse diagram. In general, a
topological sorting

[
cormen2022introduction
]
assigns a natural number to every node of a directed graph such that the number assigned to the source of an arrow is less than the number assigned to the target.

Examples 2.1

(Grading)
.

Suppose
V
is a vector space. The lattice of finite-dimensional subspaces is a lattice graded by subspace dimension. Similarly, if
X
is a set, the lattice of finite subsets is a lattice graded by cardinality. The lattice of (finite) partitions of
X
is graded by the number of parts.

Proposition 2.1
.

Suppose
\mathrm{P}
is graded with grading
r:\mathrm{P}\to\mathbb{N}
. Then,
\mathrm{P}
satisfies DCC.

Proof.

Consider a chain
C\subseteq\mathrm{P}
. Then,
r(C)
is a decreasing sequence
of natural numbers bounded below by
0
. Hence,
r(C)
converges i.e. has a minimum element.
∎

Some posets, for instance, the unit interval
[0,1]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, do not satisfy the ascending/descending chain condition. For instance, the sequence
\{a\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=1-1/n
is an ascending chain in
[0,1]
, yet
a_{n+1}
is strictly greater than
a_{n}
for every
n\geqslant 0
. However, the least upper bound of
\{a_{n}\}
provides an answer to the question:
what does
a_{n}
converge to?
In general, least upper bounds and greatest lower bounds equip posets with notions of convergence as well as algebraic properties.

5.3
Completeness

Suppose
S\subseteq\mathrm{P}
is an arbitrary subset. If they exists, we may compute least upper bounds and greatest lower bounds which we hereafter call
joins
and
meets
respectively:

\displaystyle\bigvee S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min\{y\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\succcurlyeq x\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ x\in S\};

\displaystyle\bigwedge S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\{y\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\preccurlyeq x\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ x\in S\}.

We say that
\mathrm{P}
is a
complete lattice
if for every subset
S\subseteq\mathrm{P}
,
\bigvee S
exists.
\mathrm{P}
is
directed complete
if
\bigsqcup V
exists for every directed subset
V
.
3
3

3

Of course, there is a dual notion of filtered complete, but, for reasons of tradition, directed complete posets are studied. Also for historical reasons, joins of directed sets are written with
\bigsqcup
.
In fact, it is enough that a poset contains either arbitrary meets or arbitrary joins in order to be a complete lattice, explaining the alternative nomenclature
suplattices
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Suppose
(\mathrm{P},\preccurlyeq)
is a poset and
S\subseteq\mathrm{P}
an arbitrary subset. Then,
\bigvee S
exists if and only if
\bigwedge S
exists.

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle\bigvee S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge\{y\in\mathrm{P}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\succcurlyeq x,\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ x\in S\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge\bigcap_{x\in S}\uparrow\!{x}.

∎

Warning 2.1
.

Going forward, we make the following abuse of notation. If
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
are partial orders, we do not always distinguish between the order relation on
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
, writing
\preccurlyeq
for both of them. We also do not distinguish between joins/meets in
\mathrm{P}
and joins/meets in
\mathrm{Q}
. Both we write both with the symbols
\bigvee
and
\bigwedge
.

5.4
Maps between posets

A map
f
between posets
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is
order-preserving
or
monotone
if
x\preccurlyeq y
implies
f(x)\preccurlyeq f(y)
. The set of order-preserving maps between
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
is a poset with
f\preccurlyeq G
if
f(x)\preccurlyeq g(x)
for all
x\in\mathrm{P}
.
f
is an
order embedding
if
f(x)\preccurlyeq f(y)
if and only if
x\preccurlyeq y
; an
order isomorphism
if
f
is a surjective order embedding.

The following example is relevant in explainable AI (artificial intelligence).

Example 2.3

(Binary Classification)
.

Suppose
f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,1]
is a statistical model with parameter
\theta
that inputs financial data about an applicant (e.g. income, negative debt, credit score) and outputs the likelihood that an application will not default on a load. In a supervised training,
f_{\theta}(\mathbf{x}_{i})=1
if
i
made loan payments and or
f_{\theta}(\mathbf{x}_{i})=0
if
i
defaulted. In this scenario, one would desire for
f_{\theta}
to be monotone to account for the fact that an applicant with a higher income, lower debt, and higher credit score will always be more likely to pay a loan back. This means that
f_{\theta}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant f_{\theta}(\mathbf{x})
whenever
\mathbf{x}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{y}
.

Posets, and all relations, have a duality baked into their definitions. Given
(\mathrm{P},\preccurlyeq)
, let
({\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}},\sqsubseteq)
be the poset called the
opposite poset
with the same base set
P
and order
x\sqsubseteq y
if and only if
x\succcurlyeq y
. Often in order-theoretic arguments, we invoke the
Duality Prinicple
which loosely says
if a statement is true for
\mathrm{P}
, then the dual statement obtained by dualizing all the order-theoretic definitions in the statment is true for
{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. As an example, a map
f
is
order-reversing
or
antitone
if
x\preccurlyeq y
implies
f(y)\preccurlyeq f(x)
. Alternatively, by the Duality Principle, we could say an antitone map between
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
is a monotone map
f:\mathrm{P}\to{\mathrm{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}
or, equivalently, a monotone map
f:{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Q}
.

A map
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is join-preserving if for every subset
S\subseteq\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle f(\bigvee S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee f(S).

A map
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is meet-preserving if for every subset
S\subseteq\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle f(\bigwedge S)=\bigwedge f(S).

If
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
are directed complete partial orders, then we say
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is
continuous
if for every directed subset
V\subseteq\mathrm{P}
,
f(\bigsqcup V)=\bigsqcup f(V)
.

Example 2.4

(Computer Vision)
.

Let
\mathbb{R}_{\max}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\}
denote the extended real numbers. Suppose
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\max}
is a grayscale image where
\Omega
is the image domain (e.g.
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
). In image filtering, it is popular to define filters called erosions and dilations
[
haralick1987image
]
. If is a second image called the
kernel
, define the
dilation
of
f
by

\displaystyle(f\oplus g)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{y\in\Omega}f(x-y)+g(y).

Define the
erosion
of
f
by

\displaystyle(f\ominus g)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{y\in\Omega}f(x+y)-g(y).

In the lattice of images

\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{\Omega}=\{f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\max}\},

the operators

\displaystyle\delta_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\oplus g

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\ominus g

\displaystyle\delta_{g},\varepsilon_{g}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{\Omega}

satisfy

\displaystyle\delta_{g}(\bigvee_{i}x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i}\delta_{g}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{g}(\bigwedge_{i}x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i}\varepsilon_{g}(x_{i})

for any
g\in\mathbf{L}
. Hence, dilations, as the name suggests, are join-preserving, and erosions are meet-preserving. Other filters called
openings
and
closing
are defined by composing an erosions and dilations and are useful in denoising, contrast enhancement, reconstruction and edge detection
[
maragos2009morphological
]
.

6
Fixed Points

Frequently, we study maps
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}
. Such a map is
inflationary
if
f(x)\succcurlyeq x
for all
x\in\mathrm{P}
and
deflationary
if
f(x)\preccurlyeq x
for all
x\in\mathrm{P}
. We write
\prefix(f)
and
\suffix(f)
for the subsets of deflationary and inflationary points, respectively.
Let
\fixed(f)
denote the set of fixed points
\prefix(f)\cap\suffix(f)
(anti-symmetry axiom).

6.1
(Co)closure Operators

A map
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}
is
idempotent
if
f^{2}=f
. Suppose
\mathrm{P}
is a poset and
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}
is an order-preserving map. There are various ways to characterize
\fixed(f)
depending on the properties of
\mathrm{P}
and
f
.

Definition 2.4

(Closure Operators)
.

Suppose
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}
is a map and
\mathrm{P}
is a poset. If
f
is order-preserving, inflationary, and idempotent, we say
f
is a
closure operator
. Conversely, if
g:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}
is order-preserving, deflationary, and idempotent we say
g
is a
coclosure operator
. The fixed points of a (co)closure operator are called
(co)closed
.

The theory of (co)closure operators
[
roman2008lattices
]
allows us to explicitly compute joins and meets of the fixed point lattice.

Theorem 2.1

((Co)closure)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{L}
is a complete lattice and
f:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{L}
is a closure operator on a complete lattice
(\mathbf{L}
. Then,
\fixed(f)
is a complete lattice with the following meets and joins

\displaystyle\bigsqcap_{i\in I}x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{i\in I}x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\left(\bigvee_{i\in I}x_{i}\right).

Dually, suppose
g
is a coclosure operator on
\mathbf{L}
. Then,
\fixed(f)
is a complete lattice with the following meets and joins

\displaystyle\bigsqcap_{i\in I}x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\left(\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{i\in I}x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}x_{i}.

Proof.

See
[
roman2008lattices
, Theorem 3.8]
.
∎

It is not surprising that closure operators come about naturally in topology.

Example 2.5

(Closure Spaces)
.

A
closure space
is a pair
(X,c)
where
X
is an (arbitrary) set and
c
is a closure operator
c:\wp(X)\to\wp(X)
. Closed subsets in
X
are precisely subsets
A
with
c(A)=A
. A closure space is
topological
if

\displaystyle c(A\cup B)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c(A)\cup c(B),

\displaystyle c(\emptyset)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\emptyset.

Recent work extends persistent homology to new settings (e.g. directed graphs) using closure spaces
[
bubenik2021homotopy
]
.

While (co)closure operators appear frequently throughout various branches of mathematics, the requirement of idempotency and inflation are often too stringent in practical situations.
The following fixed point theorem is integral to our study of operators on lattice-valued sheaves.

Theorem 2.2

(Tarski Fixed Point Theorem)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{L}
is a complete lattice and
f:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{L}
is an order-preserving map. Then,
\fixed(f)
is a complete lattice.

We will need a few lemmas.

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
f:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{L}
is order-preserving and
\mathbf{L}
is complete. Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a subset of
\mathbf{L}
indexed by an arbitrary set
I
. Then,

\displaystyle f\left(\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}f(x_{i}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle f\left(\bigvee_{i\in I}x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}f(x_{i}).

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

f\left(\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}\right)
is
a
lower bound of each
f(x_{i})
because
f
is order-preserving and
\bigwedge_{i}x_{i}\preccurlyeq x_{i}
. Moreover,
\bigwedge_{i\in I}f(x_{i})
is
the
greatest lower bound of the subset
\{f(x_{i})\}_{i\in I}
, implying
\bigwedge_{i\in I}f(x_{i})
precedes all other lower bounds. Hence,

\displaystyle f\left(\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}f(x_{i}).

The second statement follows from the duality principle.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}
is order-preserving. Then,
{f}^{\mathrm{op}}:{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}
defined to be the same map on sets, but under the opposite order is order-preserving as well. If
f
is join-preserving, then
{f}^{\mathrm{op}}
is meet preserving and vice versa. Furthermore,
\prefix({f}^{\mathrm{op}})=\suffix(f)
and
\suffix({f}^{\mathrm{op}})=\prefix(f)
.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

x\preccurlyeq y
in
\mathrm{P}
if and only if
x\succcurlyeq y
in
{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
f
is order-preserving implies
f(x)\preccurlyeq f(y)
in
\mathrm{P}
, or
f(x)\succcurlyeq f(y)
in
{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Hence,
{f}^{\mathrm{op}}
is order-preserving. Meets in
{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}
are equivalent to joins in
\mathrm{P}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

The classical argument
[
tarski1955lattice
]
that
\fixed(f)
is complete has three parts. First, we show
\prefix{f}\subseteq\mathbf{L}
is an invariant subset under
f
. Suppose
x\in\prefix(f)
. Then,

\displaystyle f(f(x))\preccurlyeq f(x)\preccurlyeq x

which implies
f(x)\in\prefix(f)
by monotonicity of
f
and transitivity. Next, we show that
\prefix(f)
is complete. Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\prefix(f)
. Then, Lemma
2.1
implies that
\prefix(f)
is
\bigwedge
-complete:

\displaystyle f\left(\bigwedge_{i\in I}f(x_{i})\right)\preccurlyeq\bigwedge_{i\in I}f(x_{i})

implies
\bigwedge_{i\in I}f(x_{i})\in\prefix(f)
. By Proposition
2.2
,
\prefix(f)
is also
\bigvee
-complete.
Finally, define

\displaystyle{f_{|\prefix(f)}}^{\mathrm{op}}:{\prefix(f)}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\prefix(f)}^{\mathrm{op}}.

Invariance and Lemma
2.2
imply
{f_{|\prefix(f)}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is well-defined and order-preserving on
{\prefix(f)}^{\mathrm{op}}
and that
\prefix({f_{|\prefix(f)}}^{\mathrm{op}})=\suffix\left(f_{|\prefix(f)}\right)
. Now, the argument in the second part shows
\suffix\left(f_{|\prefix(f)}\right)
is complete. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\fixed(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\suffix\left(f_{|\prefix(f)}\right)

and we are done.
∎

The proof of Theorem
2.2
implies the following corollaries.

Corollary 2.1
.

The subsets
\prefix(f)
and
\suffix(f)
are complete.

Proof.

Let
f\wedge\text{id}
be the map defined
f(x)\wedge x
on
x\in\mathbf{L}
. Then,
\fixed(f\wedge\text{id})=\suffix(f)
because
f(x)\succcurlyeq x
if and only if
f(x)\wedge x=x
. Similarly,
\fixed(f\vee\text{id})=\prefix(f)
.
∎

In particular application domains, it is only necessary to show there is a least fixed point. Hence, the Tarski Fixed Point Theorem is sometimes presented as the following corollary.

Corollary 2.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{L}
is a complete lattice and
f:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{L}
is order-preserving. Then,
f
has a least fixed point
x^{\ast}
.

Proof.

x_{\ast}=\bigwedge\fixed(f)
.
∎

7
From Posets to Lattices

Thus far, we have seen that complete lattices are posets with arbitrary meets and joints. We now present an alternative algebraic view of lattices in which we only require binary or nullary (in the case of bounded latttices) meets and joins.

Definition 2.5

(Algebraic Definition)
.

A
lattice

\mathbf{L}
is a set
L
with a pair of binary operations

\vee,\wedge:\mathbf{L}\times\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{L}

called
join
and
meet

such that for every
x,y,z\in\mathbf{L}

1.

x\vee(y\vee z)=(x\vee y)\vee z
;
x\wedge(y\wedge z)=(x\wedge y)\wedge z
(Associativity),

2.

x\vee y=y\vee z
;
x\wedge y=y\wedge z
(Commutativity),

3.

x\vee x=x
;
x\wedge x=x
(Idempotence),

4.

x\wedge(x\vee y)=x
;
x\vee(x\wedge y)=x
(Absorption).

A
bounded lattice
is a lattice with elements
0,1\in\mathbf{L}
with the axiom

5.

x\wedge 1=x
;
x\vee 0=x

A
semilattice

\mathbf{S}
is a set
S
with a binary operation

\displaystyle\wedge:\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{S}

such that for all
x,y\in\mathbf{S}

1.

x\wedge(y\wedge z)=(x\wedge y)\wedge z
(Associativity),

2.

x\wedge y=y\wedge z
(Commutativity),

3.

x\wedge x=x
(Idempotence).

A
bounded semilattice
is a semilattice with an element
1\in\mathbf{S}
with the axiom

4.

x\wedge 1=x
.

Example 2.6

(Nesting Poset)
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{M}}
is a compact smooth
2
-manifold (e.g. a closed and bounded surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
) diffeomorphic to the
2
-sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
. Suppose
f:{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Morse function
[
matsumoto2002introduction
]
factoring through an embedding and a projection onto the z-axis

i:{\mathbb{M}}\xhookrightarrow{i}\mathbb{R}^{3}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{R}

By a version of the Implicit Function Theorem
[
matsumoto2002introduction
, Theorem 2.3]
, if
t\in\mathbb{R}
is a regular value, then
f^{-1}(t)
is a compact manifold of dimension
1
. By the well-known classification of such manifolds, it follows there is a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle f^{-1}(t)\simeq\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}{\mathbb{S}}^{1},

and, furthermore,
f^{-1}
is embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Hence,
f^{-1}
is a disjoint union of Jordan curve
[
hatcher2002algebraic
]
, say,
{\mathbb{Y}}=\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{m}\}
. The complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{\mathbb{Y}}
consists of exactly
m+1
connected components, with
1
unbounded component and
m
bounded components. It follows that the set of (path) connected components
\pi_{0}({\mathbb{M}}\setminus{\mathbb{Y}})
have the structure of a join-semilattice under the following order
[
catanzaro2020moduli
]
equivalent to the circle containment order
[
scheinerman1988circle
]
.

We say two components in
\pi_{0}({\mathbb{M}}\setminus{\mathbb{Y}})
are adjacent if they they share a boundary. Then, for any pair of adjacent components
c,c^{\prime}\in\pi_{0}({\mathbb{M}}\setminus{\mathbb{Y}})
, then
\alpha_{i}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{j}
if and only if the for the corresponding (unshared) boundaries
\gamma_{i}and\gamma_{j}
, we have an inclusion of interiors
\mathrm{int}(\gamma_{i})\subseteq\mathrm{int}(\gamma_{j})
(existence, by the Jordan Curve Thoerem
[
hatcher2002algebraic
]
) or if
\mathrm{int}(\gamma_{j})
is undbounded.

On the other hand, lattices are ordered sets with binary and empty meets and joins.

Definition 2.6

(Combinatorial)
.

A
lattice
is a partially ordered set
\mathbf{L}
such that for every
x,y\in\mathbf{L}

\displaystyle x\vee y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min\left\{z\penalty\ |\penalty\ z\succcurlyeq x,\penalty\ z\succcurlyeq y\right\},

\displaystyle x\wedge y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left\{z\penalty\ |\penalty\ z\preccurlyeq x,\penalty\ z\preccurlyeq y\right\}.

A lattice is
bounded
if additionally there are elements
0,1\in\mathbf{L}
such that
0\preccurlyeq x\preccurlyeq 1
for all
x\in\mathbf{L}
.

The following proposition allows you to move freely between the order-theoretic and algebraic definitions of lattices.

Proposition 2.3
.

The algebraic (Definition
2.5
) and combinatorial (Definition
2.6
) definitions of lattices are equivalent.

Proof.

See
[
roman2008lattices
, Theorem 3.17]
.
∎

Consequently, a lattice (bounded)
\mathbf{L}
has the structure of both a (bounded)
meet-semilattice

(\mathbf{L},\wedge)
and a (bounded)
join-semilattice

(\mathbf{L},\vee)
.

There are a few specializations of lattices that we take note. A
distributive lattice
is a lattice with the property that for all
x,y,z\in\mathbf{L}

\displaystyle x\wedge(y\vee z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x\wedge y)\vee(x\wedge z),

\displaystyle x\vee(y\wedge z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x\vee y)\wedge(x\vee z).

A bounded lattice is
complemented
if for every
x\in\mathbf{L}
there exists an element
y\in\mathbf{L}
such that
x\vee y=1
and
x\wedge y=0
. Powersets (Example
2.12
) are an example of a complemented distributive lattice, also know as a
Boolean lattice
.

A
sublattice

\mathbf{K}\subseteq\mathbf{L}
is a subset
K\subseteq\mathbf{L}
closed under the
\vee
and
\wedge
operation in
\mathbf{L}
. A
quasisublattice
of
\mathbf{L}
is a lattice
\mathbf{K}
with an order embedding
i:\mathbf{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{L}
. Clearly, a sublattice is a quasi-sublattice, but not the other way around. If
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}
are complete, then we say
\mathbf{K}
is a
complete quasisublattice
.

Example 2.7

(Lattice of Subgroups)
.

Suppose
(G,\star,e)
is a group
[
dummitfoote
]
. The (normal, arbitrary) subgroups of
G
form a lattice
\subspaces{G}
with

\displaystyle M\vee N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{m\cdot n\penalty\ |\penalty\ m\in M,\penalty\ n\in N\},

\displaystyle M\wedge N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M\cap N.

The lattice
\subspaces{G}
order embeds into the powerset
\wp(G)
. However,
\subspaces{G}
is not a sublattice because the join of
M
and
N
in
\subspaces{G}
differs from the join in
\wp(G)
,
M\cup N
.

A lattice
homomorphism
is a map
f:\mathbf{K}\to\mathbf{L}
such that for all
x,y\in\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle f(x\vee y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x)\vee f(x),

\displaystyle f(x\wedge y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x)\wedge f(y).

A
bounded lattice homomorphism
is a lattice homomorphism with

\displaystyle f(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle f(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

A homomorphism
f
is an isomoprhimsm if there is another homomorphism
g:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{K}
with
g\circ f=\text{id}
and
f\circ g=\text{id}
. The reader may check that an order isomoprhism between (bounded) lattices is an isomorphism of (bounded) lattices.

Example 2.8

(Multidimensional Persistence)
.

This example was adapted from recent work on persistence modules over lattice
[
mccleary2022edit
]
. Given a lattice
\mathbf{L}
(e.g
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
representing a bifiltration), a
persistence module
is an assignment
F:\mathbf{L}\to\mathcal{V}\mathit{ec}
of lattice elements to finite-dimensional
vectorspacessuchthat
x ≼y
in
L
inducesalineartransformation
F(x)

F(x ≼y)

→
F(y)
(formally,afunctorintothecategoryofvectorspaces).A``changeinbasis^{\prime\prime}ofapersistencemodulesisalatticehomomorpism
f:
K
→
L
.Thisinducesalatticehomomorphism

^

f

:
I
K
→
I
L
.Certaininterestingfunctionsdescribingbirthsanddeathsof
i
-dimensionaltopologicalfeatures,e.g.inametricspaceorfiltrationofsimplicialcomplexes\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ghrist2008barcodes}{}{}]},arewrittenasmaps
f_i:
I
L
→
Z
whichmapaninterval
[a,b] ↦dim(Z_i F(a) ∩B_i F(b) )
where
Z_i F(a)
and
B_i F(a)
arethecyclesandboundariesofhomology\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{hatcher2002algebraic}{}{}]}.\end{example}\par

7.1
Lattices with multiplicaiton

Quite often, lattices are equipped with an algebraic structure other than binary meets and joins. For instance, the real numbers
\mathbb{R}
is an (unbounded) lattice that also has the structure of a field. We briefly recall a few definitions.

Definition 2.7

(Monoid)
.

A
monoid
is a tuple
(M,\star,e)
such that
M
is an arbitrary set,
\cdot:M\times M\to M
is an associative binary operation, and
e\in M
is an element such that
m\star e=e\cdot m=m
for all
m\in M
. A monoid is a
group
if for every
m\in M
, there exist a unique element
m^{-1}\in M
such that
m^{-1}\star m=m\star m^{-1}=e
. A monoid is
commutative
if
m\star n=n\star m
for all
m,n\in M
. A monoid is
idempotent
if
m\star m=m
for all
m\in M
.

Examples 2.2

(Monoids)
.

1.

The natural numbers with addition
(\mathbb{N},+,0)
is a monoid, but not a group. On the other hand,
(\mathbb{Z},+,0)
is a group.

2.

Suppose
X
is a set, with possible additional structure. Then, the set of maps
f:X\to X
is a monoid denoted
\End(X)
. The identity
e
is the idenity map
\text{id}:X\to X
. Multiplication is merely composition of endomorphisms.

3.

A bounded semilattice is a monid with
(\mathbf{S},\wedge,1)
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
(M,\star,e)
is a commutative idempotent monoid. Then,
M
is a semilattice.

Proof.

Define a relation
m\preccurlyeq n
if and only if
m\cdot n=m
. First check
(M,\preccurlyeq)
is a partial order with top element
e
. Then, check
m\wedge n
is in fact the element
m\cdot n
.
∎

Residuated lattices are lattices equipped with a compatible monoid structure.

Definition 2.8

(Residuated Lattice)
.

A
residuated lattice

(\mathbf{L},\wedge,\vee,0,1,\star,e,[-,-])
is a bounded lattice
(\mathbf{M},\wedge,\vee,0,1)
such that
(\mathbf{L},\cdot,e)
is a commutative monoid with a second (non-commutative) binary operation
[-,-]
on
\mathbf{M}
with the property that

\displaystyle x\star y\preccurlyeq z

if and only if

\displaystyle x\preccurlyeq[y,z]

(4)

for all
x,y,z\in\mathbf{L}
. A residuated lattice is
complete
if
\mathbf{L}
is a complete lattice.

Example 2.9

(Fuzzy Logic)
.

Consider three residuated lattice structures on the unit interval with the lattice structure
([0,1],\min,\max,0,1)
, sometimes called
t-norms
. Let
r,s,t\in[0,1]
.

1.

Suppose
s\star t=s\cdot t
is ordinary multiplication. Then,
[s,t]
is calculated

[s,t]=\begin{cases}t/s&s>t\\
1&s\leqslant t\end{cases}

from

\displaystyle r\cdot s\leqslant t

if and only if

\displaystyle r\leqslant[s,t].

2.

Define
s\star t=\min(s+t-1,0)
and check

[s,t]=\begin{cases}1-s+t&s>t\\
1&s\leqslant t\end{cases}.

3.

Define
s\star t=\min(s,t)
and check

[s,t]=\begin{cases}t&s>t\\
1&s\leqslant t\end{cases}.

Given a residuated lattice structure on
[0,1]
and a set
X
, a
fuzzy set
is a map
A:X\to[0,1]
. Viewing fuzzy sets as propositions, the logical operations of conjunction (
\wedge
), disjunction (
\vee
) and implication (
\to
) are defined pointwise according to the residuated lattice structure. For instance, if
A
and
B
are propositions,
[A,B]
is a fuzzy set with
([A,B])(s)=A(s)\to B(s)
.

Example 2.10

(Heyting Algebra)
.

Every bounded lattice is a monoid under the meet operation. However, this monoid structure does not
ipso facto
satisfy (
4
) which now reads

\displaystyle x\wedge y\preccurlyeq z

if and only if

\displaystyle x\preccurlyeq[y,z]

for all
x,y,z\in\mathbf{H}
, a
Heyting algebra
.
It follows that

\displaystyle[y,z]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee\{x\in\mathbf{H}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\wedge y\preccurlyeq z\}

leading to the definition of a
pseudocomplement

\neg x=[x,0]
. Examples of Heyting algebras are plentiful and run deep in foundation of mathematics
[
maclane2012sheaves
]
.

Example 2.11

(Max-Plus Lattice)
.

Max-plus algebra has found plentiful applications in locomotion
[
lopes2014modeling
]
, control
[
maragos2009morphological
]
, scheduling
[
cassandras2008introduction
]
, and machine learning
[
maragos2021tropical
]
. All of max-plus algebra is based on the idea of “doing linear algebra” over a residuated lattice, instead of a field. Consider the extended reals
\mathbb{R}_{\max}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
which forms a complete lattice under
\leqslant
with the additional requirement that
-\infty\leqslant t\leqslant\infty
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,
(\mathbb{R}_{\max},\wedge,\vee,-\infty,\infty,+,0,[-,-])
has the structure of a residuated lattice with
[s,t]=\max(t-s,0)
. A
max-plus
or
tropical
vector space, is then the product lattice
\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{d}
. In some setting, this product structure is called a
weighted lattice

[
maragos2017dynamical
]
because you can define “scalar multiplication” component-wise.

8
Additional Examples

Now that most of the background material on lattices is established, we dive into some more examples.

Example 2.12

(Subsets)
.

Powersets are fundamental examples of lattices to the degree that the meet and join symbols
\wedge
/
\vee
are derivates of the symbols
\cap
/
\cup
which normally represent intersection and union. Given a set
S
, the
powerset

\wp(S)
is a lattice under intersection and union.
By definition, a union of set of subsets sets is the smallest set containing every subset. Similarly, an intersection of a set of subsets is the largest set contained in each subset. If we let
\mathbf{B}=\{0M1\}
, then powersets are equivalent to the lattice of maps
S\to\mathbf{B}
, with the inherited lattice structure

\displaystyle A\wedge B(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{AND}\left(A(x),B(x)\right),

\displaystyle A\vee B(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{OR}\left(A(x),B(x)\right),

A,B\in\mathbf{B}^{S}
. Similarly, a
multiset
is a map
S\to\mathbb{N}
, often denoted
\mathbb{N}\left[S\right]
. The lattice structure on multisets is the following

\displaystyle A\wedge B(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min\left(A(x),B(x),\right)

\displaystyle A\vee B(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left(A(x),B(x),\right)

A,B\in\mathbb{N}\left[S\right]
.

An interesting use-case of powerset lattices is modeling preferences. If
X
is a set of alternatives, for instance, restaurants), then
\Rel{X}:=\wp(X\times X)
is the set of all possible preference relations on
X
. Preference relations can be combined with the meet and join axioms, in this case, intersection and union. More interesting lattice structures are obtained by restricting
\Rel{X}
to transitive-reflexive relations, as it is often argued that preferences are transitive.

Tangential to the theory of preference relations, is the theory of choice functions. A
choice function
is a map
c:\wp(X)\to\wp(X)
such that
c(A)\subseteq A
for all
A\in\wp(X)
. Intuitively, a choice function narrows down a subset of choices. It is established that the set of choice functions forms a lattice with pointwise union and intersection; interesting subclasses of choice functions form lattices as well
[
monjardet2004lattices
]
.

Example 2.13

(Subspaces)
.

Let
V
be a
vectorspace.Let
SubV
consistofthesetofallsubspacesof
V
.Ifwewrite,
Grass(V,d)
,forthe\textit{Grassmanian},the
d
-dimensionalsubspaces,thenwecanwrite\begin{aligned} \subspaces{V}&=&\coprod_{d\geqslant 0}\mathrm{Gr}(V,d).\end{aligned}
SubV
isalatticeunderthesubspaceordering
K ⩽W
if
K
isasubspaceof
W
.Thelatticeoperationsarethefollowing:
W ∨W’ = {w + w’ | w ∈W, w’ ∈W’}
oftendenoted
W + W’
;
W ∧W’ = W ∩W’
whichistriviallyasubspaceof
V
.PrincipalComponentAnalysis(PCA)anditsvariantscanbethoughtofasaprojectionofdatapointsontoanoptimalsubspace
W
ofsomefeaturespace
V
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{wright2022high}{}{}]}.\par Suppose
T: V →V
isalineartransformation.Wesayasubspace
W ⊆V
is\textit{invariant}if
T(W) ⊆W
.Forinstance,
1
-dimensionalinvariantsubspacesarespannedbyvectors
x
satisfying
T(
x
) =
λ
x
forsomescalar
λ
---theseinvariantsubpacesareeigenspaces.Itfollowsthattheinvariantsubspacesof
T
denoted
InvT
formalattice,asublatticeof
SubV
.Conditionsareknownforinvariantsubspacestobeisomorphic\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{brickman1967invariant}{}{}]}.\end{example}\par\begin{example}The set of partitions $\Pi\left(S\right)$ of a set $S$ forms a lattice. Suppose $\pi$ and $\pi^{\prime}$ are partitions. Then, the relation $\pi\preccurlyeq\pi^{\prime}$ means that \emph{$\pi$ is finer than $\pi$'} or \emph{$\pi$ is a refinement of $\pi^{\prime}$}. Put another way, for every $A\in\pi$, there is an $A^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}$ with $A\subseteq A^{\prime}$. The join $\pi\vee\pi^{\prime}$ is the finest common coarsening of $\pi$ and $\pi^{\prime}$ while $\pi\wedge\pi^{\prime}$ is the coarsest common refinement. Suppose $\pi=\{12|34|5\}$ and $\pi^{\prime}=\{123|45\}$, then $\pi\wedge\pi^{\prime}=\{12|3|4|5\}$ and $\pi\vee\pi^{\prime}=\{12345\}$. Partitions are ubiquitous in engineering, from teams of autonomous agents \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{kantaros2016distributed}{}{}]} to hierarchial clustering algorithms \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{carlsson2010characterization}{}{}]}. A {subpartition} is a partition of some subset $S^{\prime}$ of the ground set $S$. By similar reasoning, subpartitions form a lattice denoted $\Pi_{\ast}\left(S\right)$. Maps from the time domain to the lattice of subpartitions have been used to model dynamic graph with changing node sets \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{kim2018formigrams}{}{}]}.
\end{example}\par\begin{figure}\centering\includegraphics[width]{gfx/fig5.png}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{6}}{Lattice of partitions $\Pi\left(X\right)$ on $X=\{1,2,3,4\}$}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 6}}{Lattice of partitions $\Pi\left(X\right)$ on $X=\{1,2,3,4\}$}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\begin{example}The following example is due to
Recall, a random variable is a measurable map $X:\Omega\to A$ to some set $A$ which, for practical reasons, we call the {alphabet}. Depending on the context, we can think of $A$ as a set of symbols or as a set of words. The {entropy}
$$H(X)=-\sum_{w\in A}\mathbf{Pr}\left[X^{-1}\{w\}\right]\log\left(\mathbf{Pr}\left[X^{-1}\{w\}\right]\right).$$Entropy rougly represents the amount of information (bits) required to encode the outcome of $X$. A variation of entropy, relative entropy, denoted $H(Y|X)$, is the amount of information to describe the outcome of another random variable $Y$ given that the value $X$ generated is already known. Omitting some technical details \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{li2011connection}{}{}]}, random variables over a fixed probability distribution form a poset with $X\succcurlyeq Y$ if and only if $H(Y|X)=0$.
\par\par It is know the set of $A$-valued random variables under the information order forms a lattice isomoprhic to $\Pi\left(A\right)$ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{li2011connection}{}{}]}. The {information lattice} is a sublattice $\mathbf{I}$ of $\Pi\left(S\right)$ subordinate to the $\sigma$-algebra defining the underlying probability distribution. The join of random variables $X\vee Y$ is the {joint information content} between then, exactly the joint random variable $(X,Y)$. The meet $X\wedge Y$, on the other hand, is the {common information} between the two variables.
\end{example}\par

8.1
Decomposition

A recurring theme in algebra is to decompose structures into their smallest possible components. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be a lattice. An element
0\neq z\in\mathbf{L}
is join-irreducible if
z=x\vee y
implies
x=z
or
y=z
. Similarly, an element
1\neq z\in\mathbf{L}
is meet-irreducible if
z=x\wedge y
implies
z=x
or
z=y
. We denote the set of join-irreducible elements
\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{L})
.

Clearly,
(\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{L}),\preccurlyeq)
is a poset with
x\preccurlyeq y
if and only if
x\wedge y=x
. Less clearly, downsets form distributive lattices.

Theorem 2.3

(Birkhoff Duality)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{L}
is a finite distributive lattice and
\mathrm{P}
is a finite poset. Then,

\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{L}))\right)\cong\mathbf{L}

and

\mathcal{J}\left(\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{P}))\right)\cong\mathrm{P}.

Proof.

See
[
roman2008lattices
, Theorem 10.1]
.
∎

Theorem
2.3
allows us to move back and forth between these, albeit restrictive, classes of posets and lattices with ease. Because
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Q})
is a lattice of subsets (i.e. sublattice of the powerset
\wp(\mathrm{Q})
), Theorem
2.3
can also be interpreted as
every finite distributive lattice can be represented as a lattice of subsets
. In general, not restricted to finite posets and distributed lattices, the story is more complicated as illustrated by the following example.

Example 2.16

(Natural Numbers)
.

Consider the natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
with the order
m\preccurlyeq n
if and only if
n|m
, i.e. there is an
a\in\mathbb{N}
with
n=a\cdot m
.
(\mathbb{N},\preccurlyeq)
is a lattice with
m\wedge n=\mathrm{lcm}(m,n)
and
m\vee n=\mathrm{gcd}(m,n)
. First, notice that
\mathbb{N}
is distributive by the classic distributive properties of
\mathrm{gcd}
and
\mathrm{lcm}
. Hence,
(\mathbb{N},\preccurlyeq)
is an (infinite) distributive lattice. The join-irreducibles are the prime number
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{N})
with the partial order inherited by
(\mathbb{N},\preccurlyeq)
. It follows that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{N}))=\wp(\mathfrak{p})
. However, by the Fundamental Theorem of Arithmetic,
(\mathbb{N},\preccurlyeq)\cong\mathbb{N}\left[\mathfrak{p}\right]
—every natural number has a unique prime factorization. Hence,
\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{L}))\right)\ncong\mathbf{L}
. Because
\mathbb{N}
is not finite, we could not apply Birkhoff Duality.

†
†
margin:

3

Galois Connections

Suppose
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
are posets. A Galois connection is a pair of compatible order-preserving maps, one from
\mathrm{P}
to
\mathrm{Q}
, another
\mathrm{Q}
to
\mathrm{P}
, such that arbitrary joins and arbitrary meets are preserved in
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
respectively. On one hand, Galois connections are share a resemblance to linear adjoints (Definition
3.1
), but on the other hand, being order approximations of an inverse (Proposition
3.2
), share a resemblance to Moore-Penrose pseudoinverses.

9
Defining Properties

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(\mathrm{P},\preccurlyeq)
and
(\mathrm{Q},\preccurlyeq)
be posets. A
Galois connection
is a pair
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
of order-preserving maps
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
and
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }:\mathrm{Q}\to\mathrm{P}
such that for all
x\in\mathrm{P}
,
y\in\mathrm{Q}
,

\displaystyle{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq y

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle x\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y).

(5)

We call
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
a
lower-adjoint
and
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
an
upper-adjoint
.

Remark 3.1
.

The definition of a Galois connection is reminiscent of the definition of the adjoint of a linear transformation. If
T:V\to W
is a map of inner-product spaces
(V,\langle\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \rangle_{V})
,
(W,\langle\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \rangle_{W})
, then
T^{\ast}:W\to V
is the map defined by the following property

\displaystyle\langle T(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{y}\rangle_{W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{x},T^{\ast}(\mathbf{y})\rangle_{V}.

(6)

Given fixed complete lattices
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}
, we denote the set of Galois connections between these lattices
\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L})
( notation adapted from
[
grandis2013homological
]
). The set
\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L})
is a poset with the order
f\leqslant g
if and only if
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq{g}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)
and
{g}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)
for all
x,y\in\mathbf{L}
endowing it with the structure of a complete lattice. Galois connections have an equivalent definition highlighting the property that they are the “best approximation” of an order inverse.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
are posets. The following are equivalent.

1.
