({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }):\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is a Galois connection.

2.

{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
and
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }:\mathrm{Q}\to\mathrm{P}
are order-preserving, and
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\succcurlyeq\text{id}_{\mathrm{P}}
and
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\preccurlyeq\text{id}_{\mathrm{Q}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq y
if and only if
x\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)
for all
x\in\mathrm{P},\penalty\ y\in\mathrm{Q}
. Substitute
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)
for
y
. Then,
x\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)
. Alternatively, if we substitute
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)
for
x
, then we obtain
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)\preccurlyeq y
.
Conversely, suppose
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\circ{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\succcurlyeq\text{id}_{\mathrm{P}}
. If
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq y
, then, because
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is order-preserving, we have
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x))\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)
. Because
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x))\succcurlyeq x
, it follows by transitivity
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)\succcurlyeq x
. Similarly, if
x\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)
, then
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }({y}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })\preccurlyeq y
implies
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq y
.
∎

Composing lower and upper adjoints stabilizes.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
is a Galois connection as above. Then,

\displaystyle{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ };

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }.

Proof.

For the first, it suffices to show that
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\succcurlyeq{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)
for all
x\in\mathrm{P}
because
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\preccurlyeq{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
follows from Proposition
3.1
. Also, by Propositon
3.1
,
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\succcurlyeq x
.
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is order-preserving implies
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\succcurlyeq{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)
. The second is dual to the first.
∎

One consequence of the Proposition
3.2
is that
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
and
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
are idempotent.

Corollary 3.1
.

Suppose
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
is a Galois connection as above. Then,

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }

\displaystyle{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }

Another consequence of Proposition
3.2
characterizes Galois connections in which
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is an order-embedding.

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }):\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is a Galois connection and
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is an order-embedding. Then,
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }=\text{id}_{\mathrm{P}}
.

We need a lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
is a Galois connection. Then,
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is an order-embedding if and only if
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is an order-embedding.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

Suppose
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is an order-embedding. Then, by Proposition
3.2
,
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)
if and only if
x\preccurlyeq y
. This implies
x\preccurlyeq y
if and only if

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)

by Corollary
3.1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

By Proposition
3.1
, it suffices to show
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq x
for all
x\in\mathrm{P}
. By Lemma
3.1
,
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq x
if and only if

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\left({f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\right)

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x).

By Corollary
3.1
,
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)\preccurlyeq x
holds for all
x\in\mathrm{X}
.
∎

A Galois connection gives rise to a closure and coclosure operator.

Corollary 3.2
.

{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}
is a closure operator.
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }:\mathrm{Q}\to\mathrm{Q}
is a co-closure operator.

Proof.

Proposition
3.1
and Corollary
3.1

∎

We denote
\fixed({f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
by
\mathbf{Cl}(\mathbf{K})
and
\fixed({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
by
\mathbf{Cl}(\mathbf{L})
.

Definition 3.2
.

Given a connection
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }):\mathbf{K}\to\mathbf{L}
, define a poset

\displaystyle\Gal(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(x,y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ {f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x)=y,\penalty\ x={f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)\}\subseteq\mathbf{K}\times\mathbf{L}

called the
Galois lattice
of
f
.

\Gal(f)
is in fact a complete lattice.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose
f:\mathbf{K}\to\mathbf{L}
is a Galois connection between complete lattices
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}
. Then, the following are isomorphic complete lattices:
\mathbf{Cl}(\mathbf{K})
,
\mathbf{Cl}(\mathbf{L})
,
\Gal(f)
.

Proof.

For the lattice structure, Theorem
2.1
. The isomorphism follows from Proposition
3.2
.
∎

If
f=({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
is a Galois connection,
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is join-preserving and
{j}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is meet-preserving. Moreover, if
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is a join-preserving map,
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is uniquely defined. Conversely, if
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is a join-preserving map, then
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is uniquely defined.

Theorem 3.1

(Adjoint Functor Theorem
4
4

4

This result is a special case of the Adjoint Functor Theorem due to Freyd
[
freyd1964abelian
]
.
)
.

Suppose
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
are posets and
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }):\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is a Galois connection. Then,
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
preserves joins and
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
preserves preserves meets (whenever they exist). Conversely, if
f:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
is a join-preserving, there there exists a map
f^{\ast}:\mathrm{Q}\to\mathrm{P}
given by

\displaystyle f^{\ast}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee\{x\penalty\ |\penalty\ f(x)\preccurlyeq y\}

(7)

such that
(f,f^{\ast})
is a Galois connection.

It is sometimes convenient to rewrite the formula for
f^{\ast}
as

\displaystyle f^{\ast}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee f^{-1}(\downarrow y)

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

In the first part, suppose
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
is lower adjoint. We show
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
preserves joins. Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq\mathrm{P}
is an arbitrary subset of
\mathrm{P}
. Then,

\displaystyle{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\left(\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\right)\preccurlyeq y\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)

Set

y=\bigvee_{i}f(x_{i}).

Then,

\displaystyle{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\left(\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\right)\preccurlyeq\bigvee_{i}f(x_{i})\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(\bigvee_{i}f(x_{i}))

By Proposition
2.1
,
\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\preccurlyeq{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(\bigvee_{i}f(x_{i}))
holds tautologically. Hence,
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\left(\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\right)\preccurlyeq\bigvee_{i}f(x_{i})
holds. By Proposition
2.1
again,

{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(\bigvee_{i}f(x_{i}))\succcurlyeq\bigvee_{i}f(x_{i})

, leading to the equality desired.

In the second part, we show (
7
) is the upper adjoint of
f
. By Proposition
3.1
, it suffices to show
f^{\ast}f(x)\succcurlyeq x
and
ff^{\ast}(y)\preccurlyeq y
.
We have

f^{\ast}f(x)=\bigvee f^{-1}\left(\downarrow f(x)\right).

But
\{x\}\subseteq f^{-1}f(x)\subseteq f^{-1}\left(\downarrow f(x)\right)
implies

f^{\ast}f(x)=\bigvee{f}^{-1}\left(\downarrow f(x)\right)\succcurlyeq\bigvee{f}^{-1}f(x)\succcurlyeq\bigvee\{x\}=x.

For the other inequality, we have

ff^{\ast}(y)=f\left(\bigvee f^{-1}(\downarrow y)\right)\\
=\bigvee ff^{-1}(\downarrow y)

since, by the first part,
f
preserves joins. Notice,
ff^{-1}(\downarrow y)\subseteq\penalty\ \downarrow y
. Hence,

ff^{\ast}(y)=\bigvee ff^{-1}(\downarrow y)\preccurlyeq\bigvee\downarrow y=y.

∎

The dual result is the following.

Corollary 3.3
.

If
f:\mathrm{Q}\to\mathrm{P}
is meet-preserving, there exists a join-preserving map
f_{\ast}:\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{Q}
such that
(f_{\ast},f)
is a Galois connection, defined

\displaystyle f_{\ast}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge\{y\penalty\ |\penalty\ f(y)\succcurlyeq x\}.

10
Galois Connections from Relations

Some authors define Galois connections to be adjoint order-reversing maps, while others—as do we—call adjoint pairs of order-preserving maps Galois connections. We define a
contravariant Galois connection
as a pair
({f}^{\uparrow},{g}^{\downarrow})
of order-reversing maps

\displaystyle{f}^{\uparrow}:\mathbf{K}\longleftrightarrow\mathbf{L}:{f}^{\downarrow}

such that

\displaystyle{f}^{\downarrow}{f}^{\uparrow}(x)\succcurlyeq x

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle{f}^{\uparrow}{f}^{\downarrow}(y)\succcurlyeq y.

or, equivalently,

\displaystyle{f}^{\uparrow}(x)\succcurlyeq y

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{f}^{\downarrow}(y)\succcurlyeq x

Whenever there is any ambiguity, we call an order-preserving Galois connection
covariant
.
There is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}\left({\mathbf{K}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{L}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}\left({\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\right)}^{\mathrm{op}},

and we may view
({f}^{\uparrow},{f}^{\downarrow})
as an ordinary (order-preserving) Galois connection in two ways.

Caveat lector
. Although there is a one-to-one correspondence between the
sets
of join-reversing maps and meet-reversing maps, the correspondence is not an isomorphism of lattices.

There are canonical ways to construct Galois connections of each type (covariant, contravariant) from a binary relation
\mathcal{R}\subset X\times Y
. The covariant
Galois correspondence
is originally due to Schmidt
[
schmidt1953beitrage
]
, while the contravariant Galois correspondence is due to Ore
[
ore1944galois
]
.

In Chapter
, we encounter a Galois connection constructed in the above manner.

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}\in\Rel(X,Y)
is a binary relation. Then,
\mathcal{R}
induces a covariant Galois connection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wp(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wp(Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{\exists}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{\forall}}

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\exists}(U)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{y\in Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\exists x)\quad x\in U\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ x\penalty\ \mathcal{R}\penalty\ y\},

(8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\forall}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{x\in X\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\forall y)\quad x\mathcal{R}y\Rightarrow y\in V\},

(9)

U\subseteq X,V\subseteq Y
.

Proof.

First, we show that
\mathcal{R}_{\exists}
is join-preserving so we can apply Proposition
3.1
in order to compute
\mathcal{R}_{\forall}
. Let
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of subsets of
X
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\exists}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{y\in Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\exists x)x\in\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ x\mathcal{R}y\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{y\in Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\exists x\penalty\ \exists i\in I)\quad x\in U_{i}\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ x\mathcal{R}y\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I}\{y\in Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\exists x)\quad x\in U_{i}\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ x\mathcal{R}y\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{R}_{\exists}(U_{i}).

Now, we can apply the second part of Proposition
3.1
to compute
\mathcal{R}_{\forall}
. Let
V\subseteq Y
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\forall}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup\{U\subseteq X\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{R}_{\exists}(U)\subseteq V\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup\{U\subseteq X\penalty\ |\penalty\ \{y\in Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\exists x)\quad x\in U\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ x\penalty\ \mathcal{R}\penalty\ y\}\subseteq V\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{x\in X\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\forall y)\quad x\penalty\ \mathcal{R}\penalty\ y\Rightarrow y\in V\}

as desired.
∎

The following construction has lead to the field of formal concept analysis (FCA)
[
wille1982restructuring
]
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}\in\Rel(X,Y)
is a binary relation. Then,
\mathcal{R}
induces a contravariant Galois connection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\wp(X)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\wp(Y)}^{\mathrm{op}}}{}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\uparrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\downarrow}}

with

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\uparrow}(U)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{y\in Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\forall x)\left(x\in U\Rightarrow x\penalty\ \mathcal{R}\penalty\ y\right)\}

(10)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\downarrow}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{x\in X\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\forall y)\left(y\in V\Rightarrow x\penalty\ \mathcal{R}\penalty\ y\right)\},

(11)

U\subseteq X,V\subseteq Y
.

Proof.

We show
{\mathcal{R}}^{\uparrow}(U)\supseteq V
if and only if
{\mathcal{R}}^{\downarrow}(V)\supseteq U
for all
U\subseteq X,V\subseteq Y
since in
{\wp(X)}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
V\preccurlyeq V^{\prime}
if and only if
V\supseteq U^{\prime}
. Observe,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}^{\penalty\ }}^{\downarrow}={\mathcal{R}^{\dagger}}^{\uparrow}.

Hence, by simply replacing the role of
X
and
Y
, we are done.
∎

11
Integral Transforms

Recall the residual operation
[-.-]:\mathbb{L}\times\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{L}
of a residuated lattice has the defining property

\displaystyle x\star y\preccurlyeq z\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad x\preccurlyeq[y,z].

Another way of stating this property is there is a Galois connection

\displaystyle(-\star y,[y,-]):\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{L}.

We have the following lemma as consequence.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
(\mathbb{L},\wedge,\vee,0,1,\star,e,[-,-])
is a complete residuated lattice. Then,

1.

\left(\bigvee_{i}x_{i}\right)\star y=\bigvee_{i}(x_{i}\star y)
,

2.

\left[x,\bigwedge_{i}y_{i}\right]=\bigwedge_{i}\left[x,y_{i}\right]
.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
3.1
.
∎

Suppose
\mathbb{L}
is a residuated lattice, and suppose
X
is a set, possibly with additional structure. A
kernel
is a map

\displaystyle H:X\times X\to\mathbb{L}

Integration over kernels is readily defined as follows.
5
5

5

We provide a definition slightly more general than the one already introduced by Maragos
[
maragos2009morphological
]
, but less general than an analogous categorical one
[
gutierrez2010fuzzy
]
.
Suppose
f
is a map
f:X\to\mathbb{L}
and
H:X\times X\to\mathbb{L}
is a kernel. Then, the
integral transform
of
f
is the map
\hat{f}:X\to\mathbb{L}
defined

\displaystyle\hat{f}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{y\in X}H(x,y)\star f(y).

(12)

Theorem 3.4
.

Suppose
\mathbb{L}
is a residuated lattice and
H:X\times X\to\mathbb{L}
is a kernel. Then, the following forms a Galois connection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}^{X}}{}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{(-)}}

\displaystyle\left(f:X\to\mathbb{L}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\hat{f}(x)=\bigvee_{y\in X}H(x,y)\star f(y)

\displaystyle\check{g}(y)=\bigwedge_{x\in X}\left[H(y,x),g(y)\right]

\displaystyle\mapsfrom

\displaystyle\left(g:X\to\mathbb{L}\right)

Proof.

Our strategy is to show
\hat{(-)}
is join-preserving and apply Theorem
3.1
to calculate
\check{(-)}
. Suppose
f=\bigvee_{i\in I}f_{i}
where
f_{i}\in\mathbb{L}^{X}
are
\mathbb{L}
-valued functions on
\mathbb{L}
with the order
f_{i}\preccurlyeq g_{i}
if and only if
f_{i}(x)\preccurlyeq g_{i}(x)
for all
x\in X
. Then,

\displaystyle\hat{f}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{y\in X}H(x,y)\star\left(\bigvee f_{i}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{y\in X}\bigvee_{i\in I}H(x,y)\star f_{i}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}\hat{f}_{i}(x)

by Lemma
3.2
.

To calculate the right adjoint, by Theorem
3.1
,

\displaystyle\check{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee\{f\penalty\ |\penalty\ \hat{f}\preccurlyeq g\},

which implies
f
is the greatest function whose integral transform is less than
g

\displaystyle\check{g}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{f\in\mathbb{L}^{X}}\{f(y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \hat{f}(y)\preccurlyeq g(y)\quad\forall x\}.

Hence, for all
y\in X
,
f
satisfies

\displaystyle\bigvee_{x\in X}H(y,x)\ast f(x)

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle g(y).

Applying Lemma
3.2
,

\displaystyle H(y,x)\ast f(x)\preccurlyeq g(y)\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad f(x)\preccurlyeq\left[H(y,x),g(y)\right].

Therefore, tightening the bound,

\displaystyle\check{g}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{x\in X}\left[H(y,x),g(y)\right].

∎

Example 3.1

(Max-Plus Connection)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{L}
is the residuated lattice
(\mathbb{R}_{\max},\min,\max,-\infty,\infty,0,+)
with

[x,y]=y-x
if
x,y\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}\setminus\{-\infty,\infty\}
. Then, Theorem
3.4
implies

\displaystyle(A\ovee\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}a_{ij}+x_{j}

has an upper adjoint defined by
A^{\dagger}\owedge\mathbf{x}
where
A^{\dagger}
is the matrix with
a^{\dagger}_{ij}=-a_{ji}
. Hence,

\displaystyle(A^{\dagger}\owedge\mathbf{x})_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}a_{ji}-x_{j}.

Consequently, by Proposition
3.1
,

\displaystyle A^{\dagger}\owedge\left(A\ovee\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbf{x},

\displaystyle A\ovee\left(A^{\dagger}\owedge\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathbf{x},

and

\displaystyle A\ovee\mathbf{x}\leqslant\mathbf{y}\quad

if and only if

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{x}\leqslant A^{\dagger}\owedge\mathbf{y}

for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}_{\max}^{n}
.

12
Category Theory

We do no assume prior knowledge of category theory. However, as is discussed elsewhere
[
fong2019invitation
]
, there are irrefutable parallels between order theory and category theory. Reoccurring constructions pertaining to sheaves and cosheaves are further illuminated if we view them as categorical limits and colimits
[
riehl2017category
]
which, here, we present in a more friendly form- as equalizers and coequalizers.

A category
\mathcal{C}\mathit{}
consists of a collection of
objects

\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
and a collection of
morphisms
also called
arrows

\Mor{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}

such that for any two objects
x,y\in\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
, there is a collection of morphisms
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(x,y)\subseteq\Mor{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
between them with a map

\circ_{x,y,z}:\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(x,y)\times\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(y,z)\to\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(x,z)

satisfying
6
6

6

Usually, the subscripts in
\circ_{x,y,z}
are omitted.

•

(h\circ g)\circ f=h\circ(g\circ f)
(Associativity).

•

For every
x\in\Ob{C}
, there is a
\text{id}_{x}\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(x,x)
with
f\circ\text{id}=f
and
\text{id}\circ f=f
for every composable
f\in\Mor{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
(Identity).

Colloquially,
if you tell me the objects and arrows, I can tell you the category.

Examples 3.1
.

•

Suppose
(\mathrm{P},\preccurlyeq)
is a preorder. Then, we may define a category
\mathcal{P}\mathit{}
with
\Ob{\mathcal{P}\mathit{}}=\mathrm{P}
and
\Hom_{\mathcal{P}\mathit{}}(x,y)=\ast
(a one-point set) whenever
x\preccurlyeq y
in
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathit{}(x,y)=\emptyset
otherwise.

•

Suppose
(M,\star,e)
is a
monoid
. Then,
M
is a category
\mathcal{M}\mathit{}
with
\Ob{\mathcal{M}\mathit{}}=\ast
, a single object, and
\Hom_{\mathcal{M}\mathit{}}(\ast,\ast)=M
. Composition of arrows is defined according to the binary operation
\star
.

12.1
Functors

Categories are useful often because we can compare them, a perspective that has lead to connections between different branches of mathematics. Comparing two categories amounts to demonstrating a mapping between them called a functor. A
functor

F
between categories
\mathcal{C}\mathit{}
and
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
consists of a maping
F:\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}\to\Ob{\mathcal{D}\mathit{}}
and a map
F_{x,y}:\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(x,y)\to\Hom_{\mathcal{D}\mathit{}}(Fx,Fy)
such that for every
f\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(x,y)
and
g\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(y,z)
,

\displaystyle F_{x,z}(g\circ f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{y,z}(g)\circ F_{x,y}(f),

\displaystyle F(\text{id}_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{id}_{Fx}.

We have already seen functors. For instance, a functor between poset categories
F:\mathcal{P}\mathit{}\to\mathcal{Q}\mathit{}
is precisely an order-preserving map; a functor between monoid categories
F:\mathcal{M}\mathit{}\to\mathcal{N}\mathit{}
is precisely a
monoid homomorphism
. Functors also form a category in their own right. If
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
and
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
are categories, then the
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}^{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
is a category whose objects are functors
F:\mathcal{C}\mathit{}\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
whose morphisms are maps between functors called natural transformations. If
F,G:\mathcal{C}\mathit{}\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
, then a
natural transformation

\eta:F\Rightarrow G
is, roughtly, a set of morphisms
\{\eta_{x}:Fx\to Gx\}_{x\in\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}}
such that the following diagram commutes for every
f\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}(x,y)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fy}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gy}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf}

As expected, the the morphisms
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}\mathit{}^{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}}(F,G)
are, roughtly, the set of natural transformations between
F
and
G
.

12.2
(Co)equalizers

We make use of exactly one technical construction from category theory called a
(co)equalizer
. Suppose
\mathcal{C}\mathit{}
is a data category and
f,g:A\to B
are parallel morphisms from between objects
A,B\in\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
(e.g. linear transformations, join-preserving maps). By “a diagram commutes,” we mean that if you compose a sequence of arrows in the diagram to form a map from a source object to a target object, you obtain the same map as if you had composed any other sequence of arrows to go from the same source object to the same target object.

Definition 3.3

(Equalizer)
.

The
equalizer
of a parallel pair
f,g:A\to B
is an object
E
and a map
i:E\to A
such that for any map
j:D\to A
there is a unique map
j^{\prime}:D\to E
such that the following diagram commutes

Definition 3.4

(Coequalizer)
.

The
coequalizer
of a parallel pair
f,g:A\to B
is an object
Q
and a map
p:B\to Q
such that for any map
q:B\to P
there is a unique map
q^{\prime}:Q\to P
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}

12.3
(Co)products

Everyone is familiar with products of sets (cartesian products) or products of vector spaces (direct sum). These constructions often generalize in order categories.

Definition 3.5

(Product)
.

Suppose
A,B\in\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
. The
product
of
A
and
B
is an object denoted
A\prod B
with maps
p:A\prod B\to A
and
q:A\prod B\to B
called
projection maps
such that for an object
D
and maps
p^{\prime}:D\to A
and
q^{\prime}:D\to B
there is a unique map
i:D\to A\prod B
such that the diagram commutes

Definition 3.6

(Coproduct)
.

Suppose
A,B\in\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
. The
coproduct
of
A
and
B
is an object denoted
A\coprod B
with maps
i:A\to A\coprod B
and
j:B\to A\coprod B
called
injection maps
such that for an object
D
and maps
i^{\prime}:A\to D
and
j^{\prime}:B\to D
there is a unique map
p:A\coprod B\to D
such that the diagram commutes

Example 3.2

(Meets & Joins)
.

Recall that a poset
\mathrm{P}
is a category with an arrow
x\to y
between two elements (objects) if and only if
x\preccurlyeq y
. The product of two elements (if it exists) is the element
x\wedge y
defined by the universal property

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Similarly, the coproduct of
x
and
y
is the element
x\vee y
defined by the universal property

Products and coproducts of arbitrary arity, if they exist, are similarly defined. For the case of meets, we prove the following universal property from first principles.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be a complete lattice,
I
an arbirary indexing set,
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathbf{L}
, and
y\in\mathbf{L}
. Then,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}

if and only if

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{i}

for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

Suppose
y
is a lower bound of the subset
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. By definition,
\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}
is the greatest lower bound, hence,
\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}\succcurlyeq y
. Conversely, if
y\preccurlyeq\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i}
, then
y
precedes the greatest lower bound of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
implying
y
is also a lower bound by transitivity.
∎

We encourage the reader, as we develop the theory of lattice-valued sheaves and sheaf Laplacians, to imagine how one could extend results from posets, meets, joins and Galois connections to categories, products, coproducts, and adjunctions, respectively.

12.4
Adjunctions

A Galois connection is a specialization of a more general construction in category theory called an adjunction.

Definition 3.7
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}\mathit{}
and
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
are categories and
L:\mathcal{C}\mathit{}\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
and
R:\mathcal{D}\mathit{}\to\mathcal{C}\mathit{}
are functors. Then,
L
is a left adjoint (equivalently,
R
is a right adjoint) written
L\dashv R
if and only if there is a bijection

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{D}\mathit{}}(Fx,y)\leftrightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{D}\mathit{}}(x,Gy)

for all
x\in\Ob{\mathcal{C}\mathit{}}
,
y\in\Ob{\mathcal{D}\mathit{}}
.

We have already seen that if
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }):\mathbf{K}\to\mathbf{L}
are Galois connections, the
{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
and
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }
are functors. It remains to see

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathbf{L}}({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(x),y)\leftrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{K}}(x,{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }(y)).

(13)

Since
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}
are posets, hom-sets
\Hom(x,y)
is nonempty if and only if
x\preccurlyeq y
. It follows that (
13
) is equivalent to Definition
3.1
.

While we explictly showed some key properties of Galois connections hold, we, could have derived from more far more general properties of adjunctions. For instance, it is widely known that left adjoints preserve colimits, including coproducts, and right adjoints preserve limits, including products (Theorem
3.1
), or that adjoints satisfy the triangle identities (Proposition
3.2
).

.

\mathcal{S}\mathit{et}
.

2.

ndlineartransformations:
V

ec

\mathcal{H}\mathit{il}_{\mathbb{R}}
or
\mathcal{H}\mathit{il}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Posets and order-preserving maps:
\mathcal{P}\mathit{os}
.

Monoids and monoid homomorphisms:
\mathcal{M}\mathit{on}
.

Complete lattices and join-preserving maps:
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}

Complete lattices and meet-meet preserving maps:
\mathcal{I}\mathit{nf}
.

Complete lattices and Galois connections:
\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}
. The objects are complete lattices and the morphisms are pairs
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
of lower/upper adjoints. Composition is by

\displaystyle\circ

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{M})\times\Hom_{\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L})\to\Hom_{\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle({g}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{g}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })\circ({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({g}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{g}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
