4

Networks & Signals

Networks are ubiquitous, even when we do not realize it. Some examples of networks include social networks, neuronal networks, electrical networks, sensor networks, coauthorship networks, communication networks, transportion networks, and correlation networks. Often the structure of a network determines its function function
[
christakis2009connected
]
, while other times it is the other way around
[
watts2004six
]
. Dynamical systems supported on networks,
networked dynamical systems
, exhibit behavior that is more complex than than dynamical systems on their own
[
watts2004new
]
.
Signal processing
is one view of networked dynamical systems, and it is the view we adopt.

13
Networks

We model networks with graphs. Recall, a
multiset
, denoted
\mathbb{N}[S]
is a set
S
with a map
S\to\mathbb{N}
encoding the multiplicity of each element of
S
. A
directed (multi)graph
is a tuple
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
with
{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
a set of
nodes
and
{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\in\mathbb{N}[{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\times{\mathcal{V}_{G}}]
is a (multi-)set of
edges
.
7
7

7

Traditionally, a directed multigraph is called a
quiver
and, equivalently, presented as the data of
Q=({\mathcal{V}_{Q}},{\mathcal{E}_{Q}},h,t)
.
Head
and
tail
are maps
h,t:{\mathcal{E}_{Q}}\to{\mathcal{V}_{Q}}
sending an edge (arrow) to its head and tail, respectively.
An
undirected (multi)graph
is consists of the data
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
with
{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\in\mathbb{N}[{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\times{\mathcal{V}_{G}}/\sim]
where we identify the
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\times{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
with
(j,i)
by
(i,j)\sim(j,i)
. A graph is
simple
if
{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
is a set and
{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
has an empty intersection with the diagonal
\Delta({\mathcal{V}_{V}})=\{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{V}_{V}}\times{\mathcal{V}_{V}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ i=j\}
.
We hereafter assume all graphs are simple undirected graphs, unless otherwise noted. We denote nodes with lowercase letters
i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
etc. and denote edges with pairs of lowercase letters
ij,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}
etc.

In a graph, a
path

\gamma
from
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
to
j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
is a sequence of nodes
\{i=i_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}=j\}
such that
(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
; we write the legnth of
\gamma
as
|\gamma|=\ell
. The opposite path
\gamma^{-1}=(j=i_{\ell},i_{\ell-1},\dots,i_{0}=i)
is a path from
j
to
i
. A path is a
loop
if it has the same source and target nodes. The
diameter
of a graph is the length of the longest non-intersecting path
\mathrm{diam}(G)
. Paths form a category as follows.

Definition 4.1

(Free Category)
.

Suppose
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
is a category. The
free category
of
G
is a category
\Free(G)
with objects
{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
and morphisms between
i,j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
given by paths
\gamma:i\to j
. Composition of paths is concatenation written from left to right
\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}
and the identity is the trivial path denoted
\varepsilon_{i}
for each node
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
.

Suppose
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
. The
neighbors
of
i
are the set
\mathcal{N}_{i}=\left\{j\penalty\ |\penalty\ (i,j)\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\right\}
. The cardinality of
\mathcal{N}_{i}
is called the
degree
of
i
, denoted
d_{i}
. Given an edge
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, the
boundary
is the set
\partial{(i,j)}=\{i,j\}
. Given a node
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
, the
coboundary
is the (multi)set
\partial{i}=\{e\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ i\in\partial{i}\}
. For an edge
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, the
edge neighbors
consist of
\mathcal{N}_{i}=\delta{i}\cup\delta{j}-ij
. We say
i
is
incident
to
e\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
if
i\in\partial{e}
.
G
is
d-regular
if
d_{i}=d
for every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
.
G
is complete
complete
if
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
for all
i,j\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
,
i\neq j
. A graph is
connected
if for every
i,j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
, there is a path from
i
to
j
.

If
G
has
n
nodes, then square
n\times n
matrices encode graph structure with their sparsity patterns. A weighted graph is a graph
G
with weight function
\omega:{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. A weight function is frequently encoded as a
n\times n
matrix
[A]_{ij}=a_{ij}
called the
adjacency matrix
of the weighted graph

\displaystyle[A]_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\omega(i,j),&\text{ if }ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Hence, a weighted graph is given as the data
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}},A)
. The
degree matrix

[D]_{ij}=d_{ij}
of
G
is the
n\times n

\displaystyle[D]_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij},&\text{ if }i=j\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Remark 4.1

(Unweighted Graphs)
.

If a
G
is not weighted, then we set

\omega(i,j)=\begin{cases}1&ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then,
d_{ii}
is the actual degree
d_{i}
of
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
.

14
Graph Signals

Graph matrices are highly useful for answering questions about signals on graph, such as, is a signal smooth are there Graph signal processing
[
ortega2018graph
]
is concerned with
\mathbb{R}
-valued signals on graph which are functions
\mathbf{x}:{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\to\mathbb{R}
. Graph
shift operators
are matrices
S
that encode the graph topology via their sparsity structure and act on graph signals. Here are a few shift operators.

•

The
adjacency matrix

A

\displaystyle\left(A\mathbf{x}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}a_{ij}x_{j}

;

•

The
Laplacian matrix

L=D-A

\displaystyle\left(L\mathbf{x}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}a_{ij}(x_{i}-x_{j})

(14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{ii}x_{i}-\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}a_{ij}x_{j};

(15)

•

The
random walk Laplacian matrix

P=D^{-1}A

\displaystyle\left(P\mathbf{x}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{1}{d_{jj}}a_{ij}x_{j}

;

(16)

•

Matrix normalizations of the above.

In all cases, applying the graph shift operator to a graph signal results in a diffused signal. Components of the signal are integrated with the components of neighboring nodes. Iterating the shift operator repeatedly with initial condition
\mathbf{x}[0]
yields various difference equations. For instance,

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon S\mathbf{x}[t];

(17)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[0]

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n},

or,

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]-\mathbf{x}[t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon S\mathbf{x}[t];

(18)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[0]

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}

for step-size
\varepsilon>0
.

Spectral properties of the graph shift operator reflect on the nature of the diffusion process. Intricate descriptions of the diffusion based on the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
S
exist
[
chung1997spectral
]
, but coarse results are based on the following facts, for the specific case
S=L
, the graph Laplacian.

Fact
.

The time-independent solution of equation (
18
) are
\mathrm{Ker\;}L
whose dimension is equal to the number of connected components of
G
.

Proof.

Solutions of (
18
) must satisfy

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]-\mathbf{x}[t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-L\cdot\mathbf{x}[t]

implying
\mathbf{x}[t]
is in the kernel of
\mathbf{L}
.
∎

Fact
.

Suppose
\varepsilon<2d_{\max}
, the maximum degree of
G
. Then, the discrete-time linear time-invariant system (
18
) is globally exponentially stable.

Proof.

Let
P=1-\varepsilon L
. The graph Laplacian is easily seen to be positive semi-definite. Hence, every eigenvalue is non-negative and has no complex part. By Gershgorin’s Theorem
[
horn2012matrix
]
, the eigenvalues of
L
lie in the union of closed disks

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{z\penalty\ |\penalty\ |z-[L]_{ii}|\leqslant\sum_{i\neq j}|[L]_{ij}|\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}.

Since the eigenvalues are real non-negative, we can conclude that the eigenvalues of
L
are between
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 2d_{\max}
. If
\lambda_{i}
is the
i
th
eigenvalue of
L
, then
\mu_{i}=1-\varepsilon\lambda_{i}
is the
i
th
eigenvalue of
P
. Hence, if
\varepsilon<1/2d_{\max}
, then

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-1/2d_{\max}\cdot\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 1-1/2d_{\max}\cdot 2d_{\max}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

Since the eignenvalues of
P
have magnitude less than one, we conclude from elementary linear systems theory
[
chen1984linear
]
the system
18
is globally exponentially stable.
∎

Together, these imply the graph Laplacian diffusion process stabilizes at
locally constant signals
, signals constant on connected components of the graph.

Graph signal processing
[
ortega2018graph
]
harnesses graph diffusion in order to filter graph signals at different scales. (Linear)
graph filters
are linear transformations represented by matrices

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}h_{k}S^{k}

Filters act on signals by way of
\mathbf{z}=H\mathbf{x}
. Symmetric graph shift operators admit an orthonormal decomposition
S=V{\Lambda}V^{\top}
where
\Lambda
is a diagonal matrix of eigenvalues and
V
is a column matrix of eigenvectors. Thus, maybe the best way to see what filters “do” is via the
graph Fourier transform
:
\hat{\mathbf{x}}=V^{\top}\mathbf{x}
. The upshot is that
filters
are component-wise determined by polynomials
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}h_{k}\lambda_{i}^{k}
in the Fourier domain for each eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
of
S
.

14.1
Higher-order networks

Although this work is focused on ordinary networks, we can’t help but mention higher-order networks because the sheaf Laplacian presented in Chapter
7
is defined on these objects as well, as even the unimaginative reader may ascertain.

A
simplicial complex

{\mathbb{X}}
is a collection of subsets of a node set
{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
such that if
\sigma\in{\mathbb{X}}
and
\tau\subseteq\sigma
, then
\tau\in{\mathbb{X}}
. As a consequence, individual nodes
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
are elements of
{\mathbb{X}}
. Simply put, a simplicial complex is a
set closed under taking subsets
. An a single subset is called
simplex
(plural:
simplicies
). Simplices are graded by dimension
\dim\sigma=|\sigma|+1
. Let
{\mathbb{X}}_{k}
denote the set of
\sigma\in{\mathbb{X}}
such that
|\sigma|=k+1
. These subsets are called
k-simplices
. Simplices, as do graph, have boundaries and coboundaries consisting of
\partial(\sigma)=\{\tau\in{\mathbb{X}}_{\dim\sigma-1}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \tau\subsetneq\sigma\}
and
\delta(\sigma)=\{\beta\in{\mathbb{X}}_{\dim\sigma+1}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \beta\supsetneq\sigma\}
.

Simplicial complexes, like graphs, are purely combinatorial objects, but they can be realized as subsets of Euclidean space, much as graphs can be embedded. The
standard

n
-simplex is the space
\Delta[k]=\left\{(t_{0},\dots,t_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sum_{i}t_{i}=1,\penalty\ t_{i}\geqslant 0\penalty\ \forall i\right\}
. The
geometric realizaton
of
{\mathbb{X}}
is the topological space
|{\mathbb{X}}|
formed by gluing together standard simplices. In this way, we can interpret a simplicial complex as a collection of points, line segments, filled-in triangles, filled-in tetrehedra, and so forth. It is natural to consider face relations here instead of simple incidence relations. We write
\sigma\trianglelefteqslant\tau
if
\sigma\subseteq\tau
and
|\tau|=|\sigma|+1
. The face relation poset
(\Face({\mathbb{X}}),\trianglelefteqslant)
is the transitive reflexive closure of the face relation. A simplicial complex
{\mathbb{X}}
is
orientated
if every
k
-simplex is endowed with an ordering
\tau=[i_{0}i_{1}\dots i_{k}]
. If
\sigma\trianglelefteqslant\tau
, then, neccesarily,
\sigma
consists of the data of a cyclic permutation of
\{i_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
. We say the
sign
of
\sigma
is the parity of this permuation.

Hypergraphs generalize simplicial complexes, are far more practical, yet less understood. Hypergraphs model higher-order networks where relationships between agents are not required to be pair-wise (e.g. group chats), and a relationship between a number of agents does not necessarily mean that each subset of agents also share a relationship on their own. A
hypergraph

H
is a tuple
({\mathcal{V}_{H}},\mathcal{H}_{H})
where
\mathcal{H}_{H}\subseteq\wp({\mathcal{V}_{G}})
. As such, a hypergraph inherits the partial order on
\wp({\mathcal{V}_{G}})
bestowing a hypergraph with the structure of a poset
(\mathcal{H}_{H},\subseteq)
. A simplicial complex, then, is synonymous with a down-closed hypergraph.

Finally, (regular) cell complexes are another generalization of simplicial complexes of a more topological flavor. Let
\mathbb{D}^{k}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|x\|\leqslant 1\}
be the unit disk (ball) of dimension
k
. A
regular cell complex
is constructed from sequence of topological spaces

\displaystyle\emptyset={\mathbb{X}}_{-1}\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}_{0}\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}_{2}\subseteq\cdots

such that
{\mathbb{X}}_{k}
is obtained from
X_{k-1}
by “gluing”copies of
\mathbb{D}^{k}
to
{\mathbb{X}}_{k}
. For more facts about simplicial complexes and cell complexes consult a textbook on algebraic toplology
[
hatcher2002algebraic
]
. Parity of faces as well as face relations port from simplicial complexes to this slightly more general setting.

15
Representations of (Hyper)networks

We discuss various order-theoretic and other combinatorial representation of graphs, hypergraphs and simplicial complexes

1.

Incidence poset
. Suppose
G
is a graph. Incidence forms a relation on the nodes
G
denoted with the symbol
\trianglelefteqslant
according to the rule:
i\trianglelefteqslant e
if
i\in\partial e
. For each edge
ij
, ther eare exactly two incidence relations
i\trianglelefteqslant ij\trianglerighteqslant j
. The transitive-reflexive closure of
\trianglelefteqslant
completes the incidence relation to a partial order on the union
{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\cup{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
which is denoted, in abuse of notation,
\mathrm{P}_{G}
. We call this poset the
incidence poset
of
G
denoted
\mathrm{P}_{G}

2.

Face relation poset
. More generally, the simplices (cells) of a simplicial complex
{\mathbb{X}}
(regular cell complex) form a relation with
\sigma\trianglelefteqslant\tau
if and only if
\sigma\in\partial\tau
. The transitive reflexive closure of this relation is a poset denoted
\Face({\mathbb{X}})
.

Hypergraphs are notoriously unwieldy due to their lack of scalability and uniformity. Hence, it is popular to
represent
hypergraphs with other structures, such as (undirected) graphs.

3.

Clique expansion
. The clique expansion of
H
is the graph
G_{c}
with
{\mathcal{V}_{G_{c}}}={\mathcal{V}_{H}}
and
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G_{c}}}
if and only if there exists a hyperedges contianing both
i
and
j
.

4.

Line graph
. The line graph of
H
is the graph
G_{\ell}
with
{\mathcal{V}_{G_{\ell}}}=\mathcal{H}_{H}
and
ee^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}_{G_{\ell}}}
if and only if
e\cap e^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

5.

Star espansion
. The star expansion is the bipartite graph
G_{s}
with
{\mathcal{V}_{G_{s}}}={\mathcal{V}_{H}}\cup\mathcal{H}_{H}
and
(i,e)\in{\mathcal{E}_{G_{s}}}
if and only if
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{H}}
,
e\in\mathcal{H}_{H}
and
i\in e
.

A
hypergraph signal
is simply a map
\mathbf{x}:{\mathcal{V}_{H}}\to\mathbb{R}
. One benefit of these graph representations is that they unleash a variety of ways to process hypergraph signals via graph signal processing.

We turn our attention to various order-theoretic approachs to representing hypergraphs. The lattice of down-closed sets
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{H})
is a finite distributive lattice. If a hypergraph is a simplicial complex, then every hyperedge is down-closed, hence included in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{H})
.
The data of the star expansion is equivalent to the data of a binary
membership relation

\mathcal{I}\subseteq{\mathcal{V}_{H}}\times\mathcal{H}_{H}
with
i\mathcal{I}e
if and only if node
i\in e
. The Galois lattice
\Gal(\mathcal{I})
, then, offers another lattice representaiton of a hypergraph. Of note, the Galois lattice
\mathrm{Gal}(\mathcal{I})
is isomorphic to the lattice of subsets
\sigma
of nodes such that the intersection of the set of hyperedges containing every node in
\sigma
is again
\sigma
. Alternatively
\mathrm{Gal}(\mathcal{I})
is isomorphic to the lattice of subsets
\tau
of hyperedges such that the set of hyperedges containing every node in the joint intersection of nodes belonging to
\tau
is again
\tau
.

†
†
margin:

5

Sheaves

Sheaf theory is the mathematical study of the “local to global.” This is why it is not surprising that sheaf theory has been applied in settings where local data (behavior, information) extends to global data
[
ghrist2017positive
,
moy2020path
]
. In the general setting, sheaves are indexed by a poset of open sets. Recall, a
{\mathbb{X}}
be a topological space. Then, the open subsets of
{\mathbb{X}}
, denoted
\Open({\mathbb{X}})
, is a poset with inclusion
(\Open({\mathbb{X}}),\subseteq)
. Then, a sheaf valued in a data category
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
over a topological space
{\mathbb{X}}
is a contravariant functor

\underline{\mathcal{F}}:{\Open({\mathbb{X}})}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}

In this chapter, we discuss a combinatorial version of sheaf theory called
network sheaf theory
. Instead of data being stored over open sets, it is stored over nodes and edges of an undirected graph. More details on general sheaf theory are found in Appendix
.

16
Network Sheaves & Cosheaves

Sheaves are a powerful data structure for interpreting multi-agent systems with near-arbitrary flexibility as far as the type of information/data each agent retrieves and exchanges with her its neighbors. Sheaves encode assignments of data to a geometric or topological structure. Examples of structures that one can build sheaves over are many but some common ones are graphs, simplicial complexes, cell complexes, posets, topological spaces, sites and varieties. While some of these “sheaf theories” are subtle,
network sheaf theory
is not.

Definition 5.1

(Network Sheaves & Cosheaves)
.

Suppose
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
is a graph,
\mathrm{P}_{G}
its incidence poset, and
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
a data category. A
network sheaf
is a functor
\underline{\mathcal{F}}:\mathrm{P}_{G}\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
. A
network cosheaf
is a functor
\overline{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathrm{P}_{G}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
.

A sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
assigns a
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)
in
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
called a
stalk
to every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
, a
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)
to every edge
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, and
restriction maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)}

A cosheaf
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
assigns a
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i)
in
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
called a
stalk
to every node
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
, a
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)
to every
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, and
corestriction maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{F}}(ij\trianglerighteqslant i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{F}}(ij\trianglerighteqslant j)}

A
local (co)section
is a partiular element
x_{i}\in\underline{\mathcal{F}}
or
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i)
.

If a (co)sheaf is a class (data structure), than a
(co)chain
in an instantiation of a (co)sheaf.

Definition 5.2

(Cochains)
.

Suppose
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is a sheaf over
G
valued in a data category with products. The
cohains
of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
are the product

\displaystyle C^{\bullet}(\underline{\mathcal{F}};G)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\sigma\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\cup{\mathcal{E}_{G}}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma)

We call the projection of
C^{\bullet}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
onto nodes the
0-cochains

\displaystyle C^{0}(\underline{\mathcal{F}};G)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)

and the projection onto edges the
1-cochains

\displaystyle C^{1}(\underline{\mathcal{F}};G)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij).

Informally, (co)chains are sometimes called assignments to (co)sheaves
[
robinson2020assignments
]
. A (co)chain is like a rough vector field
[
lee2013smooth
]
on a manifold in that tangent vectors in a neighborhood need not vary smoothly or continuously.
.
One of the successes of sheaf theory is the formalization of local-to-global phenomena. Historically, sheaf theory
[
bredon2012sheaf
]
has been motivated by the problem of extending (continuous, smooth, holomorphic, rational) functions defined on open sets in a (topological, manifold, complex manifold, variety) space to larger open sets. In the specialized case of network sheaves, the
local-to-global
problem asks
if a
0
-cochain extend to a
1
-cochain?
In the affirmative, a
0
-cochian is said to be consistent.

Definition 5.3

(Sections)
.

Suppose
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is a sheaf and
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is a cosheaf over
G
. A
section
of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is an assignment
\mathbf{x}\in C^{\bullet}(\underline{\mathcal{F}};G)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{i\trianglelefteqslant ij}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})

(19)

agreeing over every edge
ij
. The collection of sections of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
are denoted
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
.

The following example illustrates, even in a very simple example, that restriction maps as well as the topology of the graph determine whether or not consistent assignments exist.

Example 5.1

(Constant Sheaf)
.

Let
G
be an arbitrary graph. Let
V
be a vector space. The (linear)
constnat sheaf
denoted
\underline{V}
is a sheaf valued in
\mathcal{V}\mathit{ec}
with the following data

\displaystyle\underline{V}(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\underline{V}(ij)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\underline{V}_{i\trianglelefteqslant ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{id}_{V}.

Of course the constnat sheaf makes sense in other data categories. It is the network sheaf with a particular object over every stalk and identity restriction maps.

Example 5.2

(A Twisted Sheaf)
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
be the sheaf over a 3-clique (Figure
7
). In order for a
0
-cochain to extend to a
1
-cochain,

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-x_{j}

for every edge
ij
. However, if agreement is reached over two edges, then, agreement is impossible over the remaining edge because the assignemnts to its endpoints are the same. Hence, the only global section is the zero vector
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
.

Figure 7:
A twisted sheaf.

How do you calculate sections of a sheaf? An orientation on
G
is a pair of maps

\displaystyle(-)_{+,-}:{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\to{\mathcal{V}_{G}},

sending an edge
ij
to its positive and negative endpoints
{ij}_{+}\cup{ij}_{-}=\{i,j\}
. Given a choice of orientation, sections are equivalent to the following equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{+}}

(20)

where
\delta_{-},\delta_{+}
are defined component-wise

\displaystyle(\delta_{-}\mathbf{x})_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}({ij}_{-}\trianglelefteqslant ij),

\displaystyle(\delta_{+}\mathbf{x})_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}({ij}_{+}\trianglelefteqslant ij).

The maps
\delta_{-,+}
are called
coboundary maps
. The equalizer condition specifies that
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is the largest subobject of
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
such that

\displaystyle(\delta_{-}\mathbf{x})_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\delta_{+}\mathbf{x})_{ij}

for every
\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. In summary,

Proposition 5.1
.

\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)\cong H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

An
additive category
is a data category in which maps have the structure of an abelian group; maps can be added, subtracted, and there is a zero map between any two objects.

Example 5.3

(Additive Category)
.

Suppose data category
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
is an additive category. Then, the equalizer (
20
) can be rewritten as the equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{+}-\delta_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

(21)

Then,
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\cong\mathrm{Ker\;}(\delta_{+}-\delta_{-})
.

The consequence of the above example is that calculating cohomology, at least in the category of vector spaces, reduces to calculating the kernel of linear transformation. Of course, many information systems exhibit vector-valued data, but others exhibit other data types. Returning to the equalizer (
20
), if the data category is not additive, there is no clear path forward. We cannot write the equalizers as a kernel becuase each restriction map in a
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
-valued network sheaf has no additive inverse. Hence, standard practices of linear and homological algebra are of no avail. We have already encountered categories that fail to be additive: the category
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
of complete lattices and join-preserving maps, as well as the category
\mathcal{I}\mathit{nf}
of complete lattices and meet-preserving maps.

17
Homology & Cohomology

In the past 20 years, applied algebraic topology
[
ghrist2014elementary
]
has flourished into a mature research community. Key to this development is
persistent homology
and software computing it
[
otter2017roadmap
]
. Suppose
{\mathbb{X}}
is a simplicial complex. Then, the
homology of
{\mathbb{X}}
in degree
k
, denoted
H_{k}({\mathbb{X}})
, is a vector space whose basis elements correspond to independent connected components (
k=0
), cycles (
k=1
), voids (
k=2
), and higher dimensional versions of these (
k>2
). The difficulty with using homology directly to understand data is that it is not robust to perturbations. This leads to the notion of persistent homology introduced in the early 2000s
[
edelsbrunner2000topological
]
. A filtration of simplicial complexes (spaces) is a collection of subcomplexes (subspaces)
\{{\mathbb{X}}\}_{t\in\mathrm{P}}
indexed by some poset
\mathrm{P}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}_{s}\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}_{t}

whenever

\displaystyle s\preccurlyeq t

in
\mathrm{P}
. Homology is functorial in the sense that continuous function between space
f:{\mathbb{X}}\to{\mathbb{Y}}
induces a linear transformation between vector spaces
f:H_{k}({\mathbb{X}})\to H_{k}({\mathbb{X}})

[
hatcher2002algebraic
]
. Hence, the inclusion maps of a filtration induce a series of maps
H_{k}({\mathbb{X}}_{s})\to H_{k}({\mathbb{X}}_{t})
whenever
s\preccurlyeq t
. Filtrations indexed by a chain
\mathrm{C}
have point-wise decomposition into subobjects called a barcode
[
oudot2017persistence
]
which has been highly successful in summarizing the topology of data
[
ghrist2008barcodes
]
.

We have not said anything about how homology or persistent homology is actually computed. This is because network sheaves have cohomology, a dualization of homology. Cohomology is defined in a general algebraic setting, but is equivalent to homology in field coeficients by the usual isomoprhism between a vector space and its dual.

Definition 5.4

(Cochain Complex)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
is a category with a zero map. A
(bounded) cochain complex
is a sequence of objects
C^{\bullet}=\{C^{k}\}_{k\geqslant 0}
with maps between them called
coboundary maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{3}}

such that
\delta^{k+1}\delta^{k}=0
for every
k\geqslant 0
.

If
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
has kernels, (normal) images, and quotients (See
[
grandis2013homological
]
), the
cohomology
of
C^{\bullet}
is

\displaystyle H^{k}(C^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker\;}(\delta^{k})/\mathrm{Im\;}(\delta^{k-1}).

18
Hodge Laplacians

Motivated by certain problems in multi-agent systems
[
olfati2007consensus
]
, decentralized algorithms for computing homology of simplicial complexes have been proposed
[
muhammad2006control
]
. In principle, in order to compute
H_{k}({\mathbb{X}})
, a
k
-simplex (e.g. a node) can pass messages with neighboring
k
-simplices and eventually converge to a homology vector. These algorithms, while promising, have not been widely adopted. The concept, more broadly, of distributed computation of cohomology is pertinent to the study of sheaves, objects more conducive to distributive algorithms than unstructured simplicial complexes.

The following definition goes at least as far back as Eckmann
[
eckmann1944harmonische
]
. Suppose
C^{\bullet}
is a complex in the category of real (or complex) Hilbert spaces. Then, the
combinatorial Hodge Laplacian
is the linear transformation

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bullet}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle C^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet}

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta}^{\ast}\circ\delta+\delta\circ\delta^{\ast}

(22)

The Hodge Laplacian integrates cochains in
C^{k}
with cochains in
C^{k-1}
and
C^{k+1}
, suggesting the following dynamical system, as a natural generalization of the
heat equation

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Delta_{k}\mathbf{x},

(23)

with initial condition
\mathbf{x}(0)\in C^{k}
.

Moreover, the interpretation of the stable points of the
heat equation
is justified by the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1

(Hodge Theorem)
.

Suppose
(C^{\bullet},\delta^{\bullet})
is a cochain complex of inner-product spaces. Then,

\displaystyle H^{k}(C^{\bullet})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta^{k}).

We provide a proof for the convenience of the reader.

Proof of the Hodge Theorem.

For ease of notation, let
Z_{k}=\mathrm{Ker\;}(\delta^{k})
and
B_{k}=\mathrm{Im\;}(\delta^{k-1})
, called the
cocycles
and
coboundaries
, respectively. The Hodge Laplacian decomposes into a sum of symmetric, and hence positive semi-definite, linear transformations

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}^{+}+\Delta_{k}^{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta^{k}}^{\ast}\delta^{k}+\delta^{k-1}{\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast}

called the
upper-
and
lower-

Hodge Laplacians
.
Recall, by definition of quotient vector spaces, in the category of finite-dimensional inner-product spaces

\displaystyle H^{k}(C^{\bullet})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle Z_{k}\cap B_{k}^{\bot}.

We have
x\in\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta_{k}^{+})
if and only if
\langle x,\Delta_{k}^{+}x\rangle_{C^{k}}=0
for all
x\in C^{k}
. Similarly,
x\in\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta_{k}^{-})
if and only if
\langle x,\Delta_{k}^{-}x\rangle_{C^{k}}=0
. Hence, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta_{k}^{+}+\Delta_{k}^{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta_{k}^{+})\cap\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta_{k}^{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z_{k}\cap B_{k}^{\bot}.

Thus,
x\in\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta_{k})
if and only if

\displaystyle\langle\delta^{k}x,\delta^{k}x\rangle_{C^{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\langle{\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast}x,{\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast}x\rangle_{C^{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

which holds if and only if
x\in\mathrm{Ker\;}(\delta^{k})\cap\mathrm{Ker\;}({\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast})
.

We claim
\mathrm{Ker\;}({\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast})=B_{k}^{\bot}
. First,
x\in\mathrm{Ker\;}({\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast})
if and only if

\displaystyle\langle{\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast}x,y\rangle_{C^{k-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

for every
y\in C^{k-1}
. Then,

\displaystyle\langle x,\delta^{k-1}y\rangle_{C^{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle{\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast}x,y\rangle_{C^{k-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

showing that
x
is orthogonal to every coboundary if and only
{\delta^{k-1}}^{\ast}x=0
.
∎

By basic theory of ordinary differential equations, the solution to the heat equation (
23
) is written in closed form

\displaystyle\textbf{x}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\Delta_{k}t}\textbf{x}(0).

Because
\Delta_{k}
is a positive semidefinite linear operator, the Hodge Theorem implies
x(t)
converges to a projection of
\mathbf{x}(0)
onto
H^{k}(C^{\bullet})
as
t\to\infty
. Thus, a näive algorithm for computing
H^{k}(C^{\bullet})
consists of the following steps.

1.

Select a
\mathbf{x}(0)\in C^{k}
.

2.

Compute the trajectory of the heat equation (
23
) with initial condition
\mathbf{x}(0)
.

3.

Add
\mathbf{x}(\infty)
to a growing basis of
H^{k}(C^{\bullet})
.

A strategy to compute cohomology, such as the one above, in prinicple could be parallelized because because the Hodge Laplacian is a local operator. However, it would be our guess that practical difficulties lie in numerical stability. On the other hand, calculating the entire cohomology subspace is not always practical or necessary. Applications often call for calculating a single nearest or optimal cohomology class.

18.1
Sheaf Laplacians

We are primarily interested in dynamical systems on sheaves, because, after all, the final destination for this line or research is designing, learning, and controlling multi-agent systems.
For sheaves valued in the data category of real or complex Hilbert spaces, the fundamental ingredients for a Hodge theory are present due to the following fact. For the following, suppose
{\mathbb{X}}
is a simplicial complex or regular cell complex. Then, a
cellular sheaf
valued in a category
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
is a functor
\underline{\mathcal{F}}:\Face({\mathbb{X}})\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
. As a special case, if
{\mathbb{X}}
is a graph, then a cellular sheaf over
{\mathbb{X}}
is exactly the same data as a network sheaf.

Proposition 5.2
.

Suppose
\underline{\mathcal{F}}:\Face({\mathbb{X}})\to\mathcal{H}\mathit{il}
is a sheaf of real or complex Hilbert spaces over a simplicial complex. Suppose each simplex
\sigma\in{\mathbb{X}}_{k}
is encoded as an ordered tuple. Then, the following is a cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{k}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{k}}

with

\displaystyle C^{k}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\sigma\in{\mathbb{X}}_{k}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma),

\displaystyle\left(\delta^{k}\mathbf{x}\right)_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\trianglelefteqslant\tau}{\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)}\cdot\underline{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma\trianglelefteqslant\tau)(x_{\sigma}).

Proof.

See
[
curry2014sheaves
]
.
∎

Consequently, the
sheaf cohomoloy

H^{k}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is nothing but the cohomology of the complex
C^{\bullet}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})
, and the
linear sheaf Laplacian

\displaystyle L_{k}:C^{k}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})\to C^{k}({\mathbb{X}};\underline{\mathcal{F}})

is nothing but the Hodge Laplacian of
C^{\bullet}
. Restricting, now, to the case of linear sheaves over graphs, network sheaves, the linear sheaf Laplacian (
k=0
) has has the following form

\displaystyle(L\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{i\trianglelefteqslant ij}^{\ast}\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{i\trianglelefteqslant ij}(x_{i})-\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})\right)

(24)

19
Examples of Sheaf Laplacians

The examples to follow show that, indeed, network sheaf Laplacians generalize both graph Laplacians and graph connection Laplacians, each of which, in turn, approximate smooth Laplacians on manifolds. Both the graph Laplacian and the graph connection Laplacian serve as inspiration for Laplacians of lattice-valued sheaves (Chapter
7
).

19.1
Graph Laplacians

We now revisit the graph Laplacian with a “sheafy” outlook. Suppose
{\mathbb{M}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a
k
-dimensional smooth (Riemann) manifold. Recall, a smooth manifold consists of the data of a space
{\mathbb{M}}
and a homeomorphism

\displaystyle\varphi_{U}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{k}

for every open set
U
called a
coordinate chart

such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{V}\varphi_{U}^{-1}:\varphi_{U}(U\cap V)\to\varphi_{V}(U\cap V)

is a differentiable for open sets
U\cap V\neq\emptyset
. A real-valued function
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
on an open
U
is said to be
smooth
if
f\circ\varphi_{U}^{-1}:\varphi_{U}(U)\to\mathbb{R}
is differentiable. Real-valued functions on open sets define sheaf
8
8

8

See
[
bredon2012sheaf
]
, for example

\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{M}}}
defined by the following data

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{M}}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle{\Open({\mathbb{M}})}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{V}\mathit{ec}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{M}}}(U)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{f:U\to\mathbb{R}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{M}}}(V\subseteq U)(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{|V}.

If
G
is a weighted complete graph constructed from
{\mathbb{M}}
by sampling points
\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\}
uniformly from
{\mathbb{M}}
and weighting edges inversely proportional to the distances of their boundary in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then the constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
over
G
approximates the sheaf of smooth functions on
{\mathbb{M}}
. This weighted graph is directly weighted to the following network sheaf

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(ij)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{i\trianglelefteqslant ij}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{2}}{4t}}\cdot x_{i}

where
t>0
.
The sheaf Laplacian of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is the calculated

\displaystyle(L\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left(e^{-\frac{\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{2}}{2t}}\right)^{\ast}\left(e^{-\frac{\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{2}}{2t}}x_{i}-e^{-\frac{\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{2}}{2t}}x_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}e^{-\frac{\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{2}}{t}}\right)\cdot x_{i}-\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}e^{-\frac{\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{2}}{t}}\cdot x_{j},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}a_{ij}x_{j}

We can identify graph Laplacians of arbitrary graphs as sheaf Laplacians in the same manner by setting stalks
\mathbb{R}
and defining both restriction maps over an edge
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
to be multiplication by
\sqrt{a_{ij}}
. In the above case, as the parameter
t
approaches zero, the graph Laplacain, and thus the sheaf Laplacian is shown to approximate the Laplace-Beltrami operator, a classical operator
[
lee2018introduction
]
acting on real-valued functions on
{\mathbb{M}}
(i.e. the global sections of
\mathcal{O}
, i.e. the scalar fields on
{\mathbb{M}}
). For the following, suppose
{\mathbb{M}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a
k
-dimensional maniold with Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta
, and suppose
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}
is collection of points sampled uniformly from
{\mathbb{M}}
. Set
t_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{k+2+\alpha}}
where
\alpha>0
, and let
L_{n}^{t_{n}}
be the graph Laplacian of the complete weighted graph on
{\mathcal{V}_{G}}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
with weights

\displaystyle a_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{{\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|}^{2}}{t_{n}}}.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
belkin2003laplacian
]
)
.

Suppose
f\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{M}})
. Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{t_{n}(5\pi t_{n})^{\frac{k}{2}}}L_{n}^{t_{n}}f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({\mathbb{M}})}\Delta f(x)

in probability.

19.2
Connection Laplacians

With the graph Laplacian being the first key example of a sheaf Laplacain, we introduce a second example example, the graph connection Laplacian.

Recall, some other elements from differential geometry
[
jost2008riemannian
]
. Suppose
{\mathbb{M}}
is a
k
-dimensional Riemanian manifold. To each element
x\in{\mathbb{M}}
, the
tangent space

T_{x}
is a vector space whose elements are equivalence classes of curves
\gamma:(-1,1)\to{\mathbb{M}}
with
\gamma(0)=x
whose derivatives coincide. In practice, you can visualize tangent spaces as tangent lines, tangent planes etc. The
tangent bundle
is the disjoint union
T{\mathbb{M}}=\bigsqcup_{x\in{\mathbb{M}}}
together with the projection map
T{\mathbb{M}}\xrightarrow{p}{\mathbb{M}}
. A
vector field
is a section of the tangent bundle,

s:{\mathbb{M}}\to T{\mathbb{M}}\quad p\circ s=\text{id}_{{\mathbb{M}}};

the map
s
assigns every point
x\in{\mathbb{M}}
a tangent vector
v\in T_{x}{\mathbb{M}}
. Sections of the tangent bundle define a sheaf
9
9

9

See, for instance,
[
bredon2012sheaf
]
.

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\Open({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathcal{V}\mathit{ec}

\displaystyle\Gamma(U)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{s:U\to T{\mathbb{M}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\circ s=\text{id}_{U}\}

\displaystyle\Gamma(V\subseteq U)(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{|V}

Indeed,
\Gamma(U)
is a vector space called
local sections
.
\Gamma({\mathbb{M}})
is the space of vector fields defined on the entire manifold.

Approximations of the tangent bundle lead to complications. Even if points sampled from a manifold are close, their tangent spaces will differ, in general. High-dimensional data sets are often assumed to have been sampled from low-dimensional manifold. This presents a problem because we cannot relate data in two separate local coordinates. Fortunately, this is a topic widely studied in modern differential geometry, called
parallel transport

[
knebelman1951spaces
]
. The following is a definition or a theorem depending on how you look at it. We leave technical details to the curious reader.

Definition 5.5

(
[
jost2008riemannian
]
)
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{M}}
is a
k
-dimensional manifold and
\gamma
is a smooth path
\gamma:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{M}}
with
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=y
.
Then, there is a unique invertible linear transformation

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}:T_{x}{\mathbb{M}}\to T_{y}{\mathbb{M}}

called
parallel transport
sending a tangent vector
v\in T_{x}{\mathbb{M}}
to a tangent vector
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(v)\in T_{y}{\mathbb{M}}
.

Parallel transport leads to vector diffusion and its approximated by orthogonal linear maps. The following approximates the connection Laplacian
[
atiyah1973heat
]
, a close cousin of the Laplace-Beltrami operator and a Hodge Laplacian.

Definition 5.6

(Graph Connection Laplacian
[
bandeira2013cheeger
]
)
.

Fix a dimenison
k
, and suppose
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
is a weighted graph with the data of an orthogonal linear transformation

\displaystyle O_{ij}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}

Then, the
graph connection Laplacian
is the matrix
\nabla^{2}

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{n}\to(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{n}

\displaystyle(\nabla^{2}\mathbf{v})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{j\T1\i\mathcal{N}_{i}}a_{ij}O_{ij}v_{j}

i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
.

As a consequence of an approximation result
[
singer2012vector
]
analogous to Proposition
5.2
, parallel transport between tangent spaces of the manifold are approximated in the graph connection Laplacian by orthogonal linear transformations
O_{ij}
between the tangent spaces
O_{ij}:T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{M}}\to T_{x_{j}}{\mathbb{M}}
. The graph connection Laplacian is a sheaf Laplacian. Suppose
\{O_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times K}\}_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}
is a family of orthogonal matrices indexed by the nodes of a weighted graph
G
. Construct the sheaf as follows

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{k},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{k},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{i\trianglelefteqslant ij}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{(}a_{ij})O_{i}x_{i}

Then,

\displaystyle(L\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sqrt{a_{ij}}O_{i}^{\ast}\left(\sqrt{a_{ij}}O_{i}x_{i}-\sqrt{a_{ij}}O_{j}x_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}a_{ij}O_{i}^{\ast}O_{j}x_{j}

Set
O_{ij}=O_{i}^{\ast}O_{j}
which is orthogonal.

Part II   The Tarski Laplacian

†
†
margin:

6

Lattice-Valued Sheaves

We introduce a novel class of network sheaves, study global sections of these sheaves, as well as mimick the classical sheaf operations
[
bredon2012sheaf
]
.

20
Tarski Sheaves

We begin with a definition of a sheaf over a graph whose stalks are complete and whose restriction maps are Galois connections.

Definition 6.1

(Tarski Sheaf)
.

Suppose
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
is a graph. A
Tarski sheaf
is a functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:\mathrm{P}_{G}\to\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}

A Tarski sheaf assigns a

1.

Complete lattice
\mathcal{F}(i)
to every node
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
,

2.

Complete lattice
\mathcal{F}(ij)
to every edge
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
,

3.

Galois connection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(ij)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }}

for every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
and
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
.

Remark 6.1

(Notation)
.

Slightly simplifying cumbersome notation, we will write
(\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij),\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)
for the Galois connection
({\mathcal{F}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{\mathcal{F}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
.

A Tarski bisheaf contains the data of both a sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
and a cosheaf
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
by

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{G}\to\mathcal{S}\mathit{up},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}_{G}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{I}\mathit{nf}.

by selecting restriction maps to be lower adjoints and corestriction maps to be the upper adjoints.

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose
\mathrm{P}
is a poset. Then, the following categories are equivalences of categories

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}^{\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}^{\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}^{\mathrm{P}}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\mathit{nf}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}}

Proof.

It suffices to show
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}\simeq\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}
because we obtain a natural isomorphism
\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}^{\mathrm{P}}\simeq\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}^{\mathrm{P}}
from a natural isomoprhism
\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}\simeq\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
.

\displaystyle\Pi:\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}\to\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}

is an equivalence. It is a standard fact in category theory that it suffices to show
\Pi
is full, faithful, and essentially surjective
[
riehl2017category
, Theorem 1.5.9]
. Full and faithful amounts to demonstrating a bijection

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L})\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}}(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L}).

which follows directly from Theorem
3.1
.
The argument that
{\mathcal{I}\mathit{nf}}^{\mathrm{op}}\simeq\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}
, and hence, that
\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}^{\mathrm{P}}\simeq\mathcal{I}\mathit{nf}^{{\mathrm{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
, from duality.
∎

The consequence of Theorem
6.1
is that the data of
\mathcal{F}
is equivalent to the data
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
or the data
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
.

Remark 6.2

(Bisheaves)
.

The notion of a “bisheaf” was introduced in the context of sheaves and cosheaves of abelian groups on simplicial complexes
[
macpherson2021persistent
,
nanda2020canonical
]
. Their definition of a bisheaf consists of a sheaf and a cosheaf over the same base (e.g. graph) together with a compatible homomorphism between the stalks of the sheaf to the stalks of the cosheaf. In some instances, our definition conincides with theirs. For example, if
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is a monosheaf with associated epicosheaf
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
, the
Tarski
bisheaf
(\underline{\mathcal{F}},\overline{\mathcal{F}})
is a bisheaf in the sense of McPhersen-Patel
[
macpherson2021persistent
]
.

21
New Sheaves from Old

In the following examples, sheaves of concrete objects (sets, vector spaces, hilbert spaces) will be denoted in plain font; induced Tarski sheaves will be denoted in calligraphic font.

21.1
From sheaves of sets

Suppose
S:\mathrm{P}_{G}\to\mathcal{S}\mathit{et}
is a set-valued network sheaf. Such a structure assigns the data of a set to every node and edge and (arbitrary) maps between these sets over edges. Sheaves of sets are perhaps the most basic examples of sheaves and have found their way to applications in electrical engineering, quantum theory, and topological data analysis
[
goguen1992sheaf
,
abramsky2011sheaf
,
de2016categorified
]
. The key construction is the following. Suppose
f:X\to Y
is a function. Then, there is a Galois connection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wp(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\wp(Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}(-)}

sending a subset of
X
to its image and a subset of
Y
to its preimage.
Consequently, a sheaf
F:\mathrm{P}_{G}\to\mathcal{S}\mathit{et}
factors through the powerset functor, introducing a class of Tarski sheaves

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{P}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\mathit{et}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wp(-)}

The following extended example can be safely skipped.

Example 6.1

(Reeb Graphs)
.

In topological data analysis, filtrations of a metric space by sublevel sets serve as a canonical example of a topological filtration. This example, roughly, subsumes Čech filtrations of point clouds
[
bauer2017morse
]
. The path-connected components of space is a functor
\pi_{0}:\mathcal{T}\mathit{op}\to\mathcal{S}\mathit{et}
.
\pi_{0}
, in general, does not have a group structure. Given a cell decomposition of
\mathbb{R}
with
0
-cells

Z=\{t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{r}\}

and a map
f:{\mathbb{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
, the
Reeb cosheaf
is a cosheaf valued in
\mathcal{S}\mathit{et}
with stalks
\pi_{0}f^{-1}I
for open intervals
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
subordinate to the cell decomposition and with restriction maps induced by the functoriality of
\pi_{0}

[
de2016categorified
]
. The global sections of the Reeb cosheaf correspond to path components of
{\mathbb{X}}
that are born and die at particular critical values in
Z
. Sections of the induced Tarski sheaf consist of path components that copersist.

21.2
From vector-valued sheaves

Suppose
F:\mathrm{P}_{G}\to\mathcal{V}\mathit{ec}
is a vector-valued network sheaf. Such a structure assigns a vector space to every node and edge and linear transformations between these vector spaces over edges. Sheaves of vector spaces are key to the foundations of persistent homology
[
curry2014sheaves
, Section 8.2]
. Suppose
T:V\to W
is a linear transformation. Then, there is a Galois connection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\subspaces{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\subspaces{W}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{-1}(-)}

sending a subspace of
V
to its image and a subspace of
W
to its preimage. Hence, a sheaf
F:\mathrm{P}_{G}
factors through the subspace functor
10
10

10

Grandis calls this functor the
transfer functor

[
grandis2013homological
]
, we previously called it the
Grassmanian

[
ghrist2022cellular
]
, introducing a class of Tarski sheaves

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{P}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\mathit{ec}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}\mathit{tc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subspaces{-}}

The construction is unaltered in the (full sub)category
\mathcal{H}\mathit{il}
of Hilbert spaces and linear transformations.

Example 6.2

(Constant Sheaves)
.

Suppose
V
is vector space and
\underline{V}
is the constant sheaf over
G
. Then, the induced Tarski sheaf is again the constant sheaf
\underline{\subspaces{V}}
.

22
New Sup-Lattices from Old

As a prerequisite for defining the sheaf operations for
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
-sheaves, we require categorical operations on complete lattices.

22.1
Products & coproducts

The product in
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
is inherited from the product in
\mathcal{P}\mathit{os}
. Suppose
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
are posets. Then, the
product

\mathrm{P}\times\mathrm{Q}
is the poset with the order
(x,y)\preccurlyeq(z,w)
if and only if
x\preccurlyeq y
and
y\preccurlyeq w
. The
coproduct
of
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
, denoted
\mathrm{P}\coprod\mathrm{Q}
is the poset on the disjoint union of
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
(as sets) with
x\preccurlyeq y
if
x,y\in\mathrm{P}
,
x,y\in\mathrm{Q}
, and
x\preccurlyeq y
in either
\mathrm{P}
or
\mathrm{Q}
. Immediately we run into difficulty: completeness of
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
does not imply completeness of
\mathrm{P}\coprod\mathrm{Q}
.

Frequently, we take cartesian products of sets indexed over an arbitrary set
I

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\mathbf{L}_{i}.

Elements of the product are denoted with bold letters
\mathbf{x}
. The with individual components denoted
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
. The (categorical)
product
of a family of lattices
\{\mathbf{L}\}_{i\in I}

is the cartesian product equipped with projection maps
\pi_{i}:\prod_{i\in I}\mathbf{L}_{i}\to\mathbf{L}_{i}
. Projection maps have lower and upper adjoints

\displaystyle\pi_{i}^{\ast},{\pi_{i}}_{\ast}:\mathbf{L}_{i}\to\prod_{i\in I}\mathbf{L}_{i}

(\pi_{i}^{\ast}x)_{j}=\begin{cases}x_{i}&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{cases}\quad({\pi_{i}}_{\ast}x)_{j}=\begin{cases}x_{i}&i=j\\
1&i\neq j\end{cases}

The (categorical)
coproduct
of
\{\mathbf{L}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is the cartesian product with maps
{\pi_{i}}_{\ast}:\mathbf{L}_{i}\to\coprod_{i\in I}\mathbf{L}_{i}
called inclusion maps.

Products and coproducts in
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
define the chains and cochains of a Tarski sheaf. Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a bisheaf over
G
. Then, the
0-cochains
of
\mathcal{F}
are the (complete) product lattice

\displaystyle C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)

together with the projection maps

\displaystyle\pi_{i}:C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\to\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i).

The
0-chains
of
\mathcal{F}
is the coproduct lattice

\displaystyle C_{0}(G;\overline{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\coprod_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i)

with the inclusion maps

\displaystyle{\pi_{i}}_{\ast}:\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i)\to C_{0}(G;\overline{\mathcal{F}}).

As the product and coproduct are isomorphic, then we idenity both (up to isomorphism) as the
biproduct

\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}\mathcal{F}(i)

[
riehl2017category
, Appendix E.5]
.

22.2
Tensor product & internal hom

Recall, that the set of join-preserving morphisms between two complete lattice forms a complete lattice called the
internal hom
which we hereafter denote
\left[\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L}\right]
. Suppose
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\left[\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L}\right]
. Then,

\displaystyle\left(\bigvee f_{i}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}f_{i}(x).

The
tensor product

\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{L}
is defined
11
11

11

If you prefer a definition with a universal property, see
[
joyal1984extension
]
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{L}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle{\left[\mathbf{K},{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathrm{op}}\right]}^{\mathrm{op}}.

The set
\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{L}
consists of order-reversing maps
f:\mathbf{K}\to\mathbf{L}
such that
f(\bigvee S)=\bigwedge f(S)
, or, in other words, contavariant Galois connections.

Example 6.3

(Powersets)
.

If
X
and
Y
are sets, then

\displaystyle\wp(X)\otimes\wp(Y)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\wp(X\times Y)

We have the following adjunction between the internal hom and tensor product.

Proposition 6.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L},\mathbf{M}
are complete lattices. Then,
\left[\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{L},\mathbf{M}\right]\cong\left[\mathbf{K},\left[\mathbf{L},\mathbf{M}\right]\right]
.

Proof.

See
[
joyal1984extension
, Chapter 1]
.
∎

22.3
Equalizers & coequalizers

“solving equations” in the
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
amounts to boils down to computing equalizers and coequalizers.

Definition 6.2

(Equalizer & Coequalizer)
.

An
equalizer
of a pair of parrellel join-preserving maps
f,g:\mathbf{K}\to\mathbf{L}
is a suplattice
\mathbf{E}
and map
i:\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{K}
with
f\circ i=g\circ i
such that for any map
j:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{K}
, there is a unique map
i^{\prime}:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{E}
with
i\circ i^{\prime}=j
as in the following diagram

is a suplattice
