(26)

Universal properties, however, do not suffice for actual computations.

Proposition 6.2
.

\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
has equalizers and coequalizers.

Proof.

We claim
\mathbf{E}=\{x\in\mathbf{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ f(x)=g(x)\}
with the inclusion
i
into
\mathbf{K}
is the equalizer of
f,g:\mathbf{K}\to\mathbf{L}
. First,
f\circ i=g\circ i
since
i
is an inclusion. Second,
\mathbf{E}
is complete, as we now show. Suppose
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
, then
\bigvee_{i\in I}x_{i}\in\mathbf{E}
as

\displaystyle f\left(\bigvee_{i\in I}x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}f(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}g(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\left(\bigvee_{i\in I}x_{i}\right).

For coequalizers, let
\mathbf{Q}=\{y\in\mathbf{L}\penalty\ |\penalty\ f^{\ast}(y)=g^{\ast}(y)\}
. Then, the coequalizer of
f,g:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{K}
is the lattice
{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}
with the projection
i^{\ast}:{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{L}
.
∎

23
Sections

For a
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
-sheaf, we define cohomology
H^{0}
and
H^{1}
as equalizers and coequalizers. We show
H^{0}
is isomorphic to the complete lattice of global sections. Recall,
sections
are assignments
\mathbf{x}\in C^{\bullet}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
such that that satisfy

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})\quad i\trianglelefteqslant ij\trianglerighteqslant j

(27)

for every
i\trianglelefteqslant ij\trianglerighteqslant j
.
Sections inherit the order on the product lattice
C^{\bullet}(G;\mathcal{F})
. Let
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)\subseteq C^{\bullet}(G;\mathcal{F})
denote the poset of sections. In fact,
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
is a complete lattice calculated as follows.

Definition 6.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
be a Tarski sheaf over
G
. Then, the let
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
and
H^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
to be the equalizer and coequalizer
12
12

12

See Joyal
[
joyal1984extension
, Chapter 1]
for a proof that
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
has all limits and colimits.
of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{+}}

(28)

with

\displaystyle(\delta_{-})_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}({ij}_{-}\trianglelefteqslant ij),

\displaystyle(\delta_{+})_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}({ij}_{+}\trianglelefteqslant ij).

By Proposition
6.2
,

\displaystyle H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{x}\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\delta_{-}\mathbf{x})_{ij}=(\delta_{+}\mathbf{x})_{ij}\quad\forall ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{x}\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})=\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})\quad\forall ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\}.

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{x}\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})=x_{ij}=\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right).

Theorem 6.2

(Global Sections Theorem)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
. Then,
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is a complete quasi-sublattice of
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

Warning 6.1
.

Beware,
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is not in general a sublattice.
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is an equalizer. Hence, the unique map
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\to C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is injective (see
[
riehl2017category
, Exercise 3.1.vi]
). However, joins and meets in
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
do not (in general) coincide with joins and meets in
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

.

24
Sheaf Operations

We construct four of the six classical “sheaf operations:”
tensor product sheaves, sheaf hom, pullback and pushforward
. While these four sheaf operations do reappear, we include this exposition to inspire future work.

24.1
Sheaf morphisms & constant sheaves

In order to define tensor product sheaves and sheaf hom, we first recall morphisms between sheaves. A
sheaf morphism

\underline{\mathcal{F}}\xRightarrow{\varphi}\underline{\mathcal{G}}
between
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{G}}
consists of the data of join-preserving maps
\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{F}(i)\to\mathcal{G}(i)
for every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
and join-preserving maps
\varphi_{ij}:\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)\to\underline{\mathcal{G}}(ij)
for every
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
such that for every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
and
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{G}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\circ\varphi_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{ij}\circ\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij).

(29)

The set of all sheaf morphisms is denoted
\Hom\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}},\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)
.

Proposition 6.3
.

\Hom\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}},\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)
is a complete lattice.

Proof.

Suppose
I
is an arbitrary indexing set and
\{\varphi^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
is a family of sheaf morphisms. For
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
, we write

\displaystyle\left(\bigvee_{\alpha\in I}\varphi^{\alpha}_{i}\right)(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{\alpha\in I}\varphi^{\alpha}_{i}(x_{i}),

and similarly for joins of the family
\varphi_{ij}^{\alpha}
for
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
.
It follows the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{G}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{G}}(ij)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{G}}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee\varphi_{i}^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee\varphi_{ij}^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee\varphi_{j}^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{G}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)}

commutes for all
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
because the restriction maps are join-preserving.
∎

Recall,
\mathbf{B}=\{0<1\}
, the boolean lattice with two elements. The
boolean constant sheaf
over
G
is the sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
with
\underline{\mathcal{B}}(i)=\mathbf{B}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
,
\underline{\mathcal{B}}(ij)=\mathbf{B}
for all
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, and
\underline{\mathcal{B}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)=\text{id}_{\mathbf{M}}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Suppose
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is a
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
-sheaf over a graph
G
. Then,

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom\left(\underline{\mathcal{B}},\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right).

Proof.

A sheaf morphism
\varphi
is the data of a join-preserving map
\varphi_{i}:\mathbf{B}\to\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)
for every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
and a join-preserving map
\varphi_{ij}:\mathbf{B}\to\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)
for every
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
satisfying (
29
). Therefore,
\varphi_{i}
“picks out” and
x_{i}\in\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)
for every node and
\varphi_{ij}
“picks out” a
x_{ij}\in\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)
for every edge, such that
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})=x_{ij}
. This is precisely the data of a global section.
∎

Thus, we have another proof that sections form a complete lattice. Another consequence is that the isomorphism in Proposition
6.4
implies a sheaf morphism
\underline{\mathcal{F}}\xRightarrow{\varphi}\underline{\mathcal{G}}
induces a join-preserving map on the lattice of global sections

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\Gamma(G;\varphi)}

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right).

via precomposition.

Theorem 6.3
.

Suppose
\mathrm{P}
is a poset. Then,
\Gamma(G;-)
is a functor

\Gamma(G;-):\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}^{\mathrm{P}}\to\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}.

24.2
Tensor product & sheaf hom

Definition 6.4

(Tensor Product Sheaf)
.

Suppose
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{G}}
are sheaves over a graph
G
. The
tensor product sheaf

\underline{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}
is the functor with

\displaystyle\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}(j)

\displaystyle\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)(ij)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}(ij)

for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
,
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
and restriction maps

\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)(i)\xrightarrow{\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}\left(\underline{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)(ij)

sending
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)\xrightarrow{f}{\underline{\mathcal{G}}(i)}^{\mathrm{op}}

to the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underline{\mathcal{G}}(i)}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underline{\mathcal{G}}(ij)}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathcal{G}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\underline{\mathcal{G}})(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(f)}

24.3
Pullback & pushforward

Suppose
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
and
H=({\mathcal{V}_{H}},{\mathcal{E}_{H}})
. A
graph homomorphism

p:G\to H
is an strict order-preserving map

\displaystyle p:\mathrm{P}_{G}\to\mathrm{P}_{H}.

Suppose
p
is a graph homomorphism
p:G\to H
and
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is a
\mathcal{S}\mathit{up}
-sheaf over
H
. The
pullback
of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
over
p
is the sheaf denoted
p^{\ast}\underline{\mathcal{F}}
with

\displaystyle p^{\ast}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}\left(p(i)\right)

\displaystyle p^{\ast}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}\left(p(ij)\right),

\displaystyle p^{\ast}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}\left(p(i)\trianglelefteqslant p(ij)\right).

The definition of the
pushforward
of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, denoted
p_{\ast}\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is more involved. Stalks are

\displaystyle p_{\ast}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{k\trianglelefteqslant p(ij)}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij).

The cannonical example of a pushforward is the pushforward of the map
p:G\to\ast
which is isomorphic to
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
. A canonical example of a pullback is the pullback of an embedding
H\hookrightarrow G
called the restriction of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
to
H
.

†
†
margin:

7

The Tarski Laplacian

The graph Laplacian is an operator that acts on scalar fields while the connection Laplacian acts on vector fields. Their common denominator is the sheaf Laplacian
[
hansen2019toward
]
. In this chapter, we motivate a few candidate Laplacians for Tarski sheaves from several angles. First, from the vantage of lattice-valued consensus, then as an analogy to parallel transport, then finally as an homage to Hodge theory. Along the way, we present the Hodge-Tarski Fixed Point Theorem (Theorem
7.1
) and provide algorithms to compute global sections in various settings.

25
Consensus

One practical motivation of the Tarski Laplacian is consensus.

Definition 7.1

(Multi-Agent Lattice Consensus)
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be a lattice. Suppose
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
is a graph. A
consensus
function is a map
\chi:\mathbf{L}^{{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}\to\mathbf{L}
. A
consensus protocol
is a map
\chi:\mathbf{L}^{{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}\to\mathbf{L}
(consensus function) together with an algorithm whose input is an arbitrary signal
\mathbf{x}:{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\to\mathbf{L}
and whose (desired) output is
\chi(\mathbf{x})
.

Example 7.1

(Meet-Consensus)
.

Meet-consensus takes place in a sequence of rounds. Each node, every node its state
x_{i}\in\mathbf{L},i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
to each of its neighbors
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
. At the conclusion of the round, a node
i
computes the meet of its received messages as well as (possibly)
x_{i}
. In a simple example (Figure
8
), two nodes (left, right) share a communication link with a common node (center). At
t=0
(light), each node computes meets with neighbors. At
t=2
(dark), a consensus is reached.

Figure 8:
A meet-consensus algorithm.

The updates are defined in a recursion equation

\displaystyle x_{i}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{i}[t]\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}x_{j}[t]\right)

Input:

Graph
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
;
x_{i}\in\mathbf{L},i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}

Output:

x_{i}\in\mathbf{L},i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}

repeat

for

i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}

do in parallel

for

j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}

do

x_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}\wedge x_{j}

end for

end

until

x_{i}=x_{j}\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}

Algorithm 1

Meet-consensus; in join-consensus, replace
\wedge
with
\vee
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose
G
is connected and
\mathbf{L}
is a finite lattice. The meet-consensus algorithm (
7.1
) converges to
\left(\bigwedge_{j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}x_{j}\right)_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}
in finite time.

Proof.

After one round,
x_{i}
is updated with the meet of
x_{i}
with each of the
x_{j}
for each neighbor
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}
denote the
k
-hop neighbors of
i
. It follows that

\displaystyle x_{i}[k]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}\cup i}x_{j}[0].

Because
G
is connected, for any
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
it is guaranteed that
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}\cup i\supseteq{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle x_{i}[k]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}x_{j}[0]

for
k\geqslant\mathrm{diam}(G)
.
∎

Consensus can easily be recast as a global section problem: consensus is the lattice of sections of the constant sheaf.

Proposition 7.2
.

Suppose
\underline{\mathbf{L}}
is the constant sheaf over
G
. Then,

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathbf{L}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(x_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x_{i}=x_{j}\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}\}.

Proof.

The global section condition dictates that
\text{id}(x_{i})=\text{id}(x_{j})
for all
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
.
∎

The lattice structure of
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathbf{L}}\right)
is “the same” as
\mathbf{L}
(under an isomorphism) but not equal.

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose
G
is a graph,
\mathbf{L}
is a lattice, and

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathbf{L}})\to C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathbf{L}})

(30)

\displaystyle(S\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}x_{j}

(31)

Then,

\displaystyle\suffix(S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathbf{L}}\right).

Proof.

One direction is trivial. By Proposition
7.2
, if
\mathbf{x}\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathbf{L}})
is locally constant, then
(S\mathbf{x})_{i}=x_{i}
, hence,
S\mathbf{x}\succcurlyeq\mathbf{x}
. Now suppose
\mathbf{x}\in\suffix(S)
. Then, for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
,
x_{i}=x_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}x_{j}
which is equivalent to
x_{i}\preccurlyeq\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}x_{j}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
.
Suppose
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
is a particular edge.
Then, both

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}x_{j}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{j},

\displaystyle x_{j}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}x_{i}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{i}.

Hence,
x_{i}=x_{j}
for all
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
, by the anti-symmetry axiom of posets, and
\mathbf{x}
is a global section of the constant sheaf.
∎

Beyond meet-consensus (or, dually, join-consensus), are there other consensus algorithms? To answer this question, we first describe sheaves whose sections correspond to as approximations of the constant sheaf. The following definition is due to Hansen
[
hansen2020laplacians
, Chapter 7]
.

Definition 7.2

(Approximation)
.

Let
G
be a graph and
\underline{\mathcal{G}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
be Tarski sheaves over
G
. Then,
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is an approximation to
\underline{\mathcal{G}}
if there is there is a sheaf morphism
\underline{\mathcal{F}}\xRightarrow{\varphi}\underline{\mathcal{G}}
such that
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)\cong\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j)
for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
and such that the induced join-preserving map

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{G}}\right)

is an isomorphism.

An approximation of the constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbf{L}}
consists of a sheaf whose vertex stalks are
\mathbf{L}
and whose lattice of sections is the same as
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathbf{L}}\right)
, isomorphic to
\mathbf{L}
if
G
is connected. In other words, approximations to the constant sheaf also model consensus. An approximation of
\underline{\mathbf{L}}
is a sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
characterized by the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(ij)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{ij}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)}
id
id

.

There are more questions about lattice consensus than answers. If the consensus operator
S
is a Laplacian for the constant sheaf, what is the “sheaf Laplacian” of an approximation to the constant sheaf? or an arbitrary Tarski sheaf? Furthermore, if
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
approximates
\underline{\mathbf{L}}
and
\chi:C^{0}(\underline{\mathcal{F}};G)\to\mathbf{L}
is a consensus function, does there exist “sheaf dynamics” converging to the
\chi(\mathbf{x}[0])
?
There is also a utility for consensus “in-between” meet- and join- consensus. In some applications, for instance, earliest rendezvous
[
nejad2009max
]
, min-consensus is desired. On the other hand, consider the task of forming teams amongst a set of agents. Cast as a consensus problem on the lattice of subpartitions, it would not be sensible to achieve either the finest or the coarsest organization possible.

Example 7.2

(Causal Inference)
.

Suppose a set of random variables is collected in a partial order
\mathrm{P}
. For instance, random variables could indicate (transitive, reflexive, anti-symmetric) causal relationships between features. From join-consensus on the lattice of downsets
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{P})
, a subset of
\mathrm{P}
can be recovered from the poset of join-irreducibles of
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{P})
(isomorphic to
\mathrm{P}
, Theorem
2.3
), thus defining a weak consensus function
\chi:\mathrm{P}^{n}\to\wp(\mathrm{P})
.

Example 7.3

(Distributed Optimization)
.

A multitude of agent-based tasks are formulated or equivalent to distributed optimization
[
djuric2018cooperative
]
. Suppose
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is a group of agents with objective function
f_{i},\penalty\ i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. In the simplest setting,
distributed optimzation
is unconstrained optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(\mathbf{x})

(32)

Consensus and distributed optimization are intertwined through the observation that (
33
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})

subject to

(33)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{j}\quad\forall i,j\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}

Distributed optimization, thus, reduces to consensus and optimization of the local objective functions
f_{i},i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

Recent work generalizes distributed optimization to network sheaves over
G
with euclidean stalks
[
hansen2019distributed
]
:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})

subject to

(34)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}).

where each
f_{i}
is a convex function
f_{i}:\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i)\to\mathbb{R}
.

Optimization algorithms maximizing
[
nakashima2019subspace
]
or minimizing
[
topkis1978minimizing
]
real-valued functions on lattices are restricted to certain classes of functions (e.g. submodular, supermodular, increasing difference). An algorithm solving (
34
) for
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, a Tarski sheaf, is an open problem whose solution, we speculate, will involve clever modulations between the tasks of running lattice consensus and performing “gradient” updates of the local objective functions
f_{i},i\in{\mathcal{V}_{V}}
.

26
Parallel Transport

Schreiber and Waldorf
[
schreiber2009parallel
]
and others
[
krishnan2020invertibility
]
have argued parallel transport on a manifold
{\mathbb{M}}
valued in an arbitrary category
\mathcal{D}\mathit{}
is modeled by a functor

\displaystyle P:\mathrm{Path}({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathcal{D}\mathit{}.

from the path groupoid of
{\mathbb{M}}
.
\Path({\mathbb{M}})
is a category whose objects are points from
{\mathbb{M}}
and whose morphisms are, roughly, homotopy classes of paths between points
[
hardie2000homotopy
]
. In the sequel, given a graph
G
and a Tarski sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
G
, we construct a functor

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}:\Free(G)\to\mathcal{P}\mathit{os}

sending a path
\gamma:i\to j
to a order-preserving map
P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}:\mathcal{F}(i)\to\mathcal{F}(j)
simulating parallel transport. In our view, parallel transport models message-passing of local sections of agents connected by a path in a communication network.

Definition 7.3

(Parallel Transport)
.

Suppose
G
is a graph and
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
. Suppose
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
. Then, the
parallel transport
along the path
i\to j
is the map

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{i\to j}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(i)\to\mathcal{F}(j)

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{i\to j}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i}).

If
\gamma
is an arbitrary path from
i
to
j
, then
P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}
is defined as the (unique) composition of parallel transport maps along paths of length one. The parallel transport of the trivial path
\varepsilon_{i}
based at
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
is defined to be the identity map on
\mathcal{F}(i)
.

Parallel transport is functorial by construction (i.e.
P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}}=P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma_{2}}\circ P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma_{1}}
). Furthermore, parallel transport is a functor into the catgory of posets and order-preserving maps because
P^{\mathcal{F}}_{i\to j}
, as a composition of an join- and meet- preserving map, is not (in general) join- or meet- preserving. If
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are two distinct paths from
i
to
j
, in is not the case (in general) that

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}(x_{i})=P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}(x_{j})\quad\forall i,j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}.

Whenever the above holds,
\mathcal{F}
is said to be flat.

26.1
Holonomy

Having defined parallel transport of Tarski sheaves, an abundance of potential geometric construction wait at the door including connections, curvature, characteristic classes, and more. Immediately, we are in a position to define holonomy, a construction which, roughly speaking, measures the degree to which parallel transport around a closed loop fails to preserve orientations of tangent vectors.

Definition 7.4

(Holonomy)
.

Suppose
G
is a graph. Let
\Omega_{i}(G)
denote the loops in
G
based at at particular
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
. Then, the
holonomy
of
\mathcal{F}
is the ordered monoid

\displaystyle\Hol_{i}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \gamma\in\Omega_{i}(G)\}

Holonomy is a monoid because the composition of loops yields a composition of transport maps and the trivial path
\varepsilon_{i}
yields an identity. It is a poset because
\Hol_{i}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
inherits the order of the poset of order-preserving maps
\End_{\mathcal{P}\mathit{os}}(\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i))
.

26.2
Global sections

Parallel transport characterizes sections. We first show that sections facilitate the transport of “tangent vectors” (lattice elements) to compatible "tangent vectors" (lattice elements) along any (network) path.

Lemma 7.1

(Section Path Lemma)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
and
\mathbf{x}\in\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
is a section. Then, for every path
\gamma
from
i
to
j
,

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{j}.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on path length:

Base Case.
Suppose
\gamma
is the path
i\to j
(of length one. Then, as
\mathbf{x}
is a section,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})

implies

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{j}.

Inductive Step.
Suppose
\gamma
is a path of length
\ell
. Suppose, without loss of generality, that
\gamma
is a path from
i
to
j
, and
\gamma^{\prime}
is a path from the same
i
to
j^{\prime}
. By the inductive hypothesis,
P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}(x_{i})\succcurlyeq x_{j}
. Then,

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to j^{\prime}}P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to j^{\prime}}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{j^{\prime}}.

∎

For path of length one,
neighborhood paths
, Lemma
7.1
has a partial converse.

Lemma 7.2

(Neighborhood Path Lemma)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
, and supoose

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{i\to j}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{j},\quad i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}},j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}

for all
\mathbf{x}\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. Then,
\mathbf{x}\in\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
.

Proof.

By assumption, both of the following hold for an
\mathbf{x}\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
over all
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{j},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{i}.

Therefore, because by the property of Galois connection,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i}),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j}),

which implies equality.
∎

Remark 7.1

(Proof of Lemma
7.2
)
.

The argument in the proof of the Neighborhood Lemma breaks downs when you try to generalize it to arbitrary paths. For instance, if
\gamma=(i,j,k)
is a path between distinct nodes, then

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}(k\trianglelefteqslant jk)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant jk)\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{k}

does not imply that

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}(x_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant jk)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(k\trianglelefteqslant jk)(x_{k})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{i},

only that

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant jk)\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{F}}(k\trianglelefteqslant jk)(x_{k}).

27
The Hodge-Tarski Theorem

Finally, in this section, we define a sheaf Laplacian for Tarski sheaves culminating in a Hodge-style fixed point theorem computing global sections.

27.1
The Tarski Laplacian

Definition 7.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
. The
Tarski Laplacian
is an operator defined

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle\left(L\mathbf{x}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j}).

Recall, the
meet projection
of a lattice
\mathbf{L}
onto a subset
N\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is the map

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{n}\to\mathbf{L}

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in N}x_{j}

Then, we note immediately, the Tarski Laplacian is a composition of two operations: (1) parallel transport, and (2) meet-projection. Therefore,

\left(L\mathbf{x}\right)_{i}=\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to i}(x_{j}).

(35)

Also, recall, the conditions of the Tarski Fixed Point Theorem
2.2
.

Lemma 7.3
.

The Tarski Laplacian is an order-preserving map on a complete lattice.

Proof.

By a similar argument that lattice polynomials are order-preserving
[
birkhoff1940lattice
]
,
L
is a composition of order-preserving function.
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is the product of complete lattices, hence, complete.
∎

27.2
Diffusion

For the following proposition, let
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}
denote the
k
-hop neighborhood
of
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
.
13
13

13

Let
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{0}=\{i\}
.
Suppose
W\subseteq{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
is a subset of nodes. Then,
\pi_{W}
is the projection of
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
onto
W
.

Proposition 7.4

(Locality)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
with Tarski Laplacian
L
. Suppose
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
. Then, the Tarski Laplacin is restricted to the complement of the
k
-hope neighborhood

\displaystyle L^{k}_{{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j)\to\prod_{j\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j)

is the identity.

As an operator, the Tarski Laplacian is said to be
local
in the sense that
(L^{k}\mathbf{x})_{i}
only depends only on the assignment to the nodes
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}^{k}
. Familiar examples of other local operators include the graph Laplacian, graph connection Laplacian, and the linear sheaf Laplacian.

27.3
Similarities to the connection Laplacian

Recall, in the connection Laplacian,

\displaystyle(L\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d_{i}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}w_{ij}O_{ij}x_{j},

tangent vectors of neighbors
\{\mathbf{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}
are transformed by orthogonal maps
O_{ij}
approximating parallel transport between
T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{M}}
and
T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{M}}
. Next, node messages
\{O_{ij}\mathbf{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}
are received and aggregated with via a weighted sum.

In the Tarski Laplacian (
35
), lattice elements of neighbors
\{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}
are transformed by order-preserving maps
P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to i}
subordinate to
\mathcal{F}
that model parallel transport . Next, node messages
\{P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to i}(x_{j})\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}
are received and aggregated with a meet-projection onto
\mathcal{N}_{j}
.

27.4
A fixed point theorem

Recall, the Hodge Theorem (Theorem
5.1
) characterizes the fixed points of heat equation (
23
). We now state a culminating result characterising the suffix points of the Tarski Laplacian with the sections
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
of a Tarski sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
over
G
.

Theorem 7.1

(Tarski-Hodge Theorem)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over a graph
G
with Tarski Laplacian
L:C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\to C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. Then,

\displaystyle\suffix(L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}).

(36)

The proof relies on the theory of parallel transport from Section
26
.

Proof of Tarski-Hodge Theorem.

By the Section Path Lemma (Lemma
7.1
) and the Neighborhood Lemma (Lemma
7.2
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\in\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)

if and only if

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to i}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{i}

holds for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
,
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
. By the universal property of meets (Lemma
3.3
),

\displaystyle P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to i}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{i}

for all
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}

if and only if

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}P^{\mathcal{F}}_{j\to i}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle x_{i}.

Therefore,
(L\mathbf{x})_{i}\succcurlyeq x_{i}
for every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{V}}
precisely if
\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.
∎

Equivalently, we may rewrite the above as the fixed points of the operator
L\wedge\text{id}
because
L(\mathbf{x})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{x}
if and only if
L(\mathbf{x})\wedge\mathbf{x}
.

Corollary 7.1
.

\fixed(L\wedge\text{id})=H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Remark 7.2
.

Indeed, the Hodge-Tarski Theorem (Theorem
7.1
) is a “Hodge-style fied point theorem.” The Euler discretization of the heat equation (
23
) (step-size
\alpha>0
) is the following

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]-\mathbf{x}[t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha\Delta_{k}\mathbf{x}

(37)

It follows

\displaystyle\fixed(\text{id}-\alpha\Delta)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle H^{k}(C^{\bullet})

by the isomorphism between
\mathrm{Ker\;}(\Delta_{K})
and
H^{0}(C^{\bullet})
in the Hodge Theorem (Theorem
5.1
). Then, compare (
37
) to

\displaystyle\fixed(\text{id}\wedge L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{k}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}).

(38)

A second (also obvious) corollary is a consequence of the isomorphism between global sections and
H^{0}
(Proposition
5.1
).

Corollary 7.2
.

\fixed(L\wedge\text{id})\cong\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
.

A final corollary offers a third an alternate proof to Theorem
6.2
that the global sections of a Tarski sheaf are a complete quasi-sublattice of
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
.

Corollary 7.3
.

H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is a complete lattice. Furthermore, there is an order-embedding of
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
into the complete lattice
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
of
0
-cochains.

Proof.

The Tarski Laplacian is an order-preserving map on a complete lattice
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
(Proposition
7.3
). Therefore, by Corollary
7.3
and the Tarski Fixed Point (Theorem
2.2
), the isomorphism between
\Gamma\left(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}\right)
and
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
followed by the inclusion of
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
into
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is an order-embedding.
∎

28
Towards Operator Theory

By Corollary
7.3
, global sections of Tarski sheaves have arbitrary meets and joins, forming a complete lattice. In the context of multi-agent systems, this implies the set of consistent assignments of lattice-valued data over a network of agents forms a lattice and results in two merging operations (meet/join) between consistent assignments, sections. In this section, we introduce two new operators: the closure and the Helmholtzian. We discuss a strategy for computing the explicit lattice structure of
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
of a Tarski sheaf.

28.1
A closure operator

We introduce another local operator on
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
that is
not
a Laplacian.

Definition 7.6

(Closure)
.

The
closure
is the endomorphism

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\rightarrow C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

(39)

\displaystyle\left(E\mathbf{x}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i}).

(40)

Recall, if
({f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ },{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ })
is a Galois connection, then
{f}^{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }{f}_{\,\begin{picture}(-1.0,-3.0)\circle*{3.0}\end{picture}\ }\succcurlyeq\text{id}
(Proposition
3.1
). The closure, then, “intensifies” local data. To be precise,
E
is a closure operator on the complete lattice
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Theorem 7.2

(Closure Theorem)
.

The operator
E
is a closure operator on
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Proof.

It is trivial to check
E
is order-preserving. By Proposition
3.1
and the universal property of meets (Lemma
3.3
),
(E\mathbf{x})_{i}\succcurlyeq x_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
. Hence,
E
is inflationary. To show
E
is idempotent, we show
E^{2}(\mathbf{x})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{x}
and
E^{2}(\mathbf{x})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{x}
. For the first inequality,

\displaystyle(E^{2}\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\bigl(\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})\Bigl)

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(E\mathbf{x})_{i},

where the penultimate equality follows from Lemma
3.2
. For the second inequality,

\displaystyle(E^{2}\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

by
E\mathbf{x}\succcurlyeq\mathbf{x}
and monotonicity. Hence,
E^{2}=E
.
∎

The fixed points of the closure operator
E
is a quasi-sublattice of
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Corollary 7.4
.

\fixed(E)
is a complete lattice.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
2.1
.
∎

Moreover, we can explicitly compute meets and joins with

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\wedge_{\fixed(E)}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\wedge\mathbf{y},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\vee_{\fixed(E)}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(\mathbf{x}\vee\mathbf{y}).

The following establishes a connection between the closure and the Tarski Laplacian.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose
\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. Then,
L(\mathbf{x})=E(\mathbf{x})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. Then, for all
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
,

\displaystyle(L\mathbf{x})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

as
\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})=\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})
for every
ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}
.
∎

28.2
Meets & joins of sections

Lemma
7.4
suggests that one may attempt to restrict
L
to
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
to obtain a closure operator. The following shows that
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is invariant under
L
. A similar result was shown by Cunninghame-Green & Butkovic
[
cuninghame2003equation
]
in a highly-specialized case (Figure
4
) of max-plus restriction maps over the network with two nodes and one edge.

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose
\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. Then,
L(\mathbf{x})\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Proof.

By the Hodge-Tarski Theorem (Theorem
7.1
), it suffices to show that
L(\mathbf{x})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{x}
which follows by monotonicity of
L
and transitivity.
∎

Warning 7.1
.

It would be tempting to conclude that
L|_{H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})}:H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\to H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is a closure operator, and, then, one may compute meets and joints in
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
explicitly by Theorem
2.1
. However, this argument is invalid because, in the definition of the Tarski Laplacian, the product lattice structure on
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
does not, in general, coincide with the lattice structure on
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. Hence, this argument is circular.

28.3
The Tarski Helmholtzian

The Tarski Laplacian acts on
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. We define another operator, analogous to the graph Helmholtzian
[
lim2020hodge
]
which acts on
C^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Definition 7.7

(Helmholtzian)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
. The
Tarski Helmholtzian
is the operator
\mathfrak{L}:C^{1}(G;\overline{\mathcal{F}})\to C^{1}(G;\overline{\mathcal{F}})
defined

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}\mathbf{x})_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\bigvee_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}-j}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij^{\prime})(x_{ij^{\prime}})\right)\vee\left(\bigvee_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}-i}\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant i^{\prime}j)(x_{i^{\prime}j})\right)

We conjecture that the lattice
H^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
constructed as a coequalizer (
20
) coincides with the prefix points of the Tarski Helmholtzian.

Conjecture 7.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
with Tarski-Helmholtz operator
\mathfrak{L}:C_{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\to C_{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
. Then,

\displaystyle\prefix(\mathfrak{L})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{1}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}}).

(41)

29
Algorithms

We end this chapter with a discussion of two different algorithms that are inspired by the Tarski Laplacian.

29.1
Heat Flow

The Tarski Laplacian inspires a discrete-time heat equation on
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(L\wedge\text{id})\mathbf{x}[t]

(42)

with initial condition
\mathbf{x}[0]\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.
Locally, these updates can be cast as a distributed algorithm (Algorithm
2
). In order to guarantee the algorithm terminates, we require an assumption.

Assumption 7.1
.

(DCC)
The product lattice
C^{0}(\underline{\mathcal{F}};G)
satisfies the descending chain condition (Definition
2.3
).

Input:

Bisheaf
\mathcal{F}
over graph
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
; initial
x_{i}\in\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i),i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}

Output:

x_{i}\in\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i),i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}

repeat

for

i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}

do in parallel

for

j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}

do

x_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}\wedge\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})

end for

end

until

\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})=\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j})\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ ij\in{\mathcal{E}_{G}}

Algorithm 2

Heat Flow

Proposition 7.5

(Completeness)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
satisfying Assumption
7.1
. Then, Algorithm
2
converges in finitely many iterations.

Proof.

L\wedge\text{id}\preccurlyeq\text{id}
(trivially) and the recursion
\mathbf{x}[t]=(L\wedge\text{id})^{t}\mathbf{x}[0]
imply
\mathbf{x}[s]\preccurlyeq\mathbf{x}[t]
whenever
s\leqslant t
. Hence,
\{\mathbf{x}[t]\}_{t\geqslant 0}
forms a descending chain in
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
satisfying Assumption
7.1
, then Algorithm
2
converges to some
\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.5
, the Algorithm
2
converges. Hence, there is a
t_{0}
such that
(L\wedge\text{id})^{t}\mathbf{x}[t]=(L\wedge\text{id})^{t}\mathbf{x}[t_{0}]
for all
t\geqslant t_{0}
. Therefore, by the Tarski-Hodge Theorem,
(L\wedge\text{id})^{t}\mathbf{x}[t_{0}]\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.
∎

If a poset is graded, then it satisfies the descending chain condition.

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
is graded. Then, Algorithm
2
also terminates in finite time and converges to a section.

The heat flow alogithm (Algorithm
2
) is depicted visually (Figure
9
).

Figure 9:
Heat flow on a Tarski Sheaf.

29.2
Gossip

Gosip algorithms are a general class of decentralized algorithms that are broadly characterized as having each exchange information with one or a few neighboring nodes each round. The advantages of gossip over Laplacian-based methods include fault tolerance and a relaxation of assumption that nodes update their states synchronously
[
kempe2003gossip
]
. In order to describe an asynchronous version of the Heat Flow algorithm, we will need some notation. Let
\tau:\mathbb{N}\to\wp({\mathcal{V}_{G}})
be a
broadcast sequence
. At time
t
, the nodes
\tau(t)\subseteq{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
broadcast to all neighbors
j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
. This is quite general. For instance, if
\tau(t)={\mathcal{V}_{G}}
, then we recover Algorithm
2
. However, if
\tau(t)
is a single node, then we have a truly asynchronous algorithm. Additionally, we require nodes to continually exchange information.

Assumption 7.2
.

(Liveness)
For every
i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
and
t_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
t\geqslant t_{0}
such that
i\in\tau(t)
.

The Heat Flow algorithm (Algorithm
2
) is modified (Algorithm
3
) by aggregating parallel transported lattice-valued information from only the nodes that are broadcasting at a given time.

Input:

Bisheaf
\mathcal{F}
over graph
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
; initial
x_{i}\in\mathcal{F}(i),i\in{\mathcal{V}_{G}}
; broadcast sequence
\tau:\mathbb{N}\to\wp({\mathcal{V}_{G}})
.

for

t\in\mathbb{N}

do

for

i\in\tau(t)

do

for

j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}

do

\mathtt{sendTo}(j)\leftarrow\underline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{i})

\mathtt{receiveFrom}(i)\leftarrow\mathtt{sendTo}(j)

x_{j}\leftarrow x_{j}\wedge\overline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)\left(\mathtt{receiveFrom}(i)\right)

end for

end for

end for

Algorithm 3

Gossip

Alternatively, we may rewrite the algorithm as a recursion equation

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{t}\bigl(\mathbf{x}[t]\bigr)\wedge\mathbf{x}[t]

(43)

called the
gossip equation
with the time-varying Tarski Laplacian
L_{t}
defined as follows.

Definition 7.8

(Time-Varying Tarski Laplacian)
.

Suppose
G
is a graph and
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
and
\tau:\mathbb{N}\to\wp({\mathcal{V}_{G}})
satisfies Assumption
7.2
. The
asynchronous Tarski Laplacian
:
14
14

14

If
\tau(t)\cap\mathcal{N}_{i}=\emptyset
, then
(\tilde{L}_{t})_{i}=1
because
\bigwedge\emptyset=1
.
is the time-varying operator

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{t}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}\times C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})\to C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})

(44)

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{L}_{t}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{j\in\tau(t)\cap\mathcal{N}_{i}}\overline{\mathcal{F}}(i\trianglelefteqslant ij)\underline{\mathcal{F}}(j\trianglelefteqslant ij)(x_{j}).

We now state a equilibrium result for global sections for gossip.

Theorem 7.3

(Theorem 1
[
riess2022asynchronous
]
)
.

Suppose
G
is a graph and
\mathcal{F}
is a Tarski sheaf over
G
. Suppose
\tau:\mathbb{N}\to\wp({\mathcal{V}_{G}})
is a firing sequence satisfying Assumption
7.1
. Suppose
\tilde{L}_{t}
is the asynchronous Tarski Laplacian. Then, if
\tau
is a broadcast sequence satisfying Assumption
7.2
, the sections
H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
coincide with the time-independent solutions of the recursion equations

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}[t+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(L_{t}\wedge\text{id})\mathbf{x}[t]

(45)

with initial condition
\mathbf{x}[0]\in C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

Corollary 7.7
.

Suppose
G
,
\mathcal{F}
and
\tau
satisfy Assumptions
7.1
and
7.2
. Then, Algorithm
3
terminates in a finite number of iterations and converges to an
\mathbf{x}\in H^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
.

In Appendix
, we review some experimental results on the convergence rate of the gossip algorithm (Algorithm
3
).

Part III   Applications

†
†
margin:

8

Signals

Signal processing is a broad area of engineering devoted to the study of various sorts of signals. Some examples of signals include audio, video, speech, image, communication, GPS, sonar, lidar, and radar, among others. In our view, signal processing is synonymous with information processing.

At a high level of abstraction, a signal is a map
x:\Omega\to\mathcal{C}
where
\Omega
is a domain and
\mathcal{C}
is an object in a data category. Classically,
\mathcal{C}
is a finite-dimensional vector space and each dimension of
\mathcal{C}
is called a
channel
or
feature
, however, the same terminology may be used if
\mathcal{C}
is an arbitrary object in a data category. The set of signals over
\Omega
valued in
\mathcal{C}
is denoted
\mathcal{X}(\Omega,\mathcal{C})
.

Examples 8.1
.

Some traditional examples:

Waveforms
. Suppose
T
is the period of the waveform. Then,
\Omega
is a the interval
[0,T]
with and
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{R}
(electric potential, displacement etc.).

Images
.
\Omega
is a grid
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
(pixels) and
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
(intensities) where usually
d=1
(grayscale) or
3
(RBG).

Networks
.
\Omega
is a graph
G=({\mathcal{V}_{G}},{\mathcal{E}_{G}})
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
; each channel is a feature. In practice, channels are often features generated from data and the graph is constructed from a similarity measure between nodes/agents (e.g. Pearson correlation).

In Chapter
6
we introduced lattice-valued sheaves as a way to generalize sheaves valued in sets and vector spaces. If every stalk in a lattice-valued sheaf over a graph
G
is the same lattice, say
\mathbf{L}
, then it is perfectly reasonable to identify
0
-cochains
C^{0}(G;\underline{\mathcal{F}})
with signals
\mathcal{X}({\mathcal{V}_{G}},\mathbf{L})
. We might, then, hope for some of the themes of signal processing to port to lattice-valued sheaves.

30
Algebraic Signal Processing

Algebraic signal processing (ASP) is a unified signal processing framework based on the representation theory of associative algebras
[
etingof2011introduction
]
. One limitation of ASP, however, is that
\mathcal{X}(\Omega,\mathcal{C})
must be a vector space in this perspective. We first basics of representation theory.

Definition 8.1

(Algebras)
.

Let
eafield.An\textit{associative algebra}
or
algebraforshort,isavectorspace
A
equippedwithanassociativebilinearmap\begin{aligned} \cdot:A\times A\to A\end{aligned}A
15
15

15

Equivalently, a linear map
A\otimes A\to A
algebra is
unital
if there exists an element
1\in A
with
a\cdot 1=1\cdot a=a
. An
algebra homomorphism
between algebras
(A,\cdot)
and
(A^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime})
is a linear transformation
\rho:A\to A^{\prime}
such that
\rho(a\cdot b)=\rho(a)\cdot^{\prime}\rho(b)
.

We provide several examples of algebras whose representationsa are of interest in engineering.

Example 8.1

(Polynomials)
.

Polynomials with coeficients in
ormanalgebradenoted
]
;multiplicationisgivenbymultiplicationofpolynomials.Multinomials,inthesameway,formanalgebra
_1,t_2, …, t_n]
.Theunitistheconstantpolynomial

_1,t_2, …, t_n]
